#### Abstracts
##### **Language Models Steer on Injected Uncertainty Tokens, but Fail to Self-Monitor In Situ**
2611.31228v1 by Daniel Theron

Prompting language models to verbalize stepwise numerical confidence during generation is an increasingly popular approach for triggering backtracking and error correction in agentic reasoning. This strategy presupposes two capabilities: (1) in-situ monitoring fidelity (does confidence drop when an error occurs?) and (2) downstream steering control (does an uncertainty token causally steer the model to repair its error?). We evaluated four open-weights models (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B, and Phi-4, 14.7B) across 15,200 math benchmark attempts, isolating 2,017 reasoning errors within each model's capability frontier. Applying 27,568 counterfactual continuations across these failure points, we demonstrate a consistent decoupling between monitoring and control.
  On monitoring, all four models fail to detect their own errors. Verbalized confidence does not distinguish mistake steps from sound reasoning, yielding near-chance within-position AUROCs between 0.500 and 0.561. Even at a model's first reasoning error, trajectory confidence remains saturated at 95.1% to 99.9%, with noticeable drops ($\ge 10$ points) appearing on fewer than 9% of errors in Llama and under 1.6% in the other three models. In contrast, models respond readily to external uncertainty: injecting a low-confidence tag (10%) at the error step significantly increases downstream correction across all models (+3.7 to +22.5 points over format-matched 95% certainty; all $p \le 0.0022$), reaching up to 38.5% under active rethinking instructions. However, injecting doubt onto already-correct steps causes models to discard valid reasoning, reducing solve rates by up to 22.8 points. Inference-time self-correction is thus bottlenecked by in-situ error detection rather than downstream repair capacity: models readily steer toward recovery when prompted with doubt, yet fail to self-monitor during generation.

摘要：

##### **CTLink: A Text-Prompted Segmentation Model for Linking Radiology Report Findings to 3D Chest CT Scans**
2611.31222v1 by Mohammed Baharoon, Sung Eun Kim, Mahmoud Hussain Alabbad, Lucas Bijnens, Ali Piracha, Reem Alwasiah, Talal Yahyan, Abdulrahman Alamoudi, Mashael Alshehri, Rayan Alsager, Hannah Hancock, Anna M. Hunkemöller, Peter Youssef, Mona A. Alhammad, Afnan Aljumayan, Brecht Van Berkel, Eric Bijnens, Saud Alhussain, Maarten Steyvers, Rolf Symons, Bryan Eelen, Ayed Alaklubi, Maha Buraykan Alshammari, Amirah Fahad Alshammeri, Tomas Gilvydis, Ibrahim AlTurki, Monerah Almohideb, Ali Alburkani, Shahad Alhomaiani, Fatima Jabali, Abdulrhman Aljouie, Daesung Kim, Junha Im, Michael Moritz, Hassan R. Alomaish, Pranav Rajpurkar

Radiology reports describe findings in text only, without visually marking them on the scan, so readers cannot directly see which abnormality each reported finding refers to. We introduce CTLink, a text-prompted 3D segmentation model that segments findings described in a chest CT report. CTLink was trained on 4123 noncontrast chest CT scans with 9547 segmented report findings, including ReXGroundingCTv2, a newly annotated dataset of 1134 scans and 1895 findings with every instance of each finding segmented. On the 200 ReXGroundingCT validation scans, CTLink achieved a hit rate of 0.816, an instance F1 score of 0.456, and a mean Dice score of 0.400, compared with 0.533, 0.175, and 0.234 for VoxTell, a publicly available text-prompted segmentation model (P < .001 for all). ##### **When Does Overriding the Majority Vote Help?**
2611.31165v1 by Terry Ma, Aidan Zhang

When should additional reasoning or verification replace a majority-vote answer? We call such a replacement an override. When the vote is incorrect, the correct answer is often absent from the original responses. ##### **Vetoes and missed agreements in language-model bargaining**
2611.30950v1 by Fuad Ali

A language model played three representatives, each instructed to get the best payoff for its own group. One representative requested a plan, another chose the sole offer, and all three voted. If anyone voted no, each group received its fallback payoff. In 60 games with Qwen3-Next-80B, the representative at risk of a loss always requested a plan that would leave everyone better off. In each game, two copies of the proposer saw the same payoffs, peer request and reminder that the offer needed three yes votes. Showing the proposer how many groups gained under each plan raised mutually beneficial agreements from 6/60 to 31/60 (paired increase 41.7 percentage points, conservative 95% interval 18.8 to 58.5). Two of the four plans benefited everyone, so picking uniformly would put a mutual gain on the ballot half the time on average. In an earlier study, GPT-4.1 played 12 conflict games under two message conditions. Majority rule passed 15 of 24 offers, all at one group's expense. Unanimity passed two, both mutual gains. In the Qwen games, voters never saw the counts. They approved all 37 offers that helped everyone. On all 83 offers that would have cost one group points, its representative voted no.

摘要：

##### **Aggregation Rules Induced by KL-Regularised Preference Dynamics on Networks**
2611.30941v1 by Milan Vojnovic

We study agents that each align a model to their own preference data while observing one another, and ask which aggregation rule the resulting policies implement. Each round, an agent takes one KL-regularised step. The step involves a reference that the agent is penalised for leaving and a challenger against which its responses are compared, and each of the two may be the agent's own or an average over the agents it observes; attention need not be reciprocal. Aggregating the reference makes the rule collective. Agent $v$ then has the weight $π_v/β_v$, its share of the network's long-run attention divided by its temperature. If, in addition, the challenger is fixed, the agents concentrate on the response maximising $\sum_v(π_v/β_v)r_v(y)$, where $r_v$ is agent $v$'s reward, and the rule is cardinal. If the reference is fixed and the challenger aggregated, the agents' policies at a fixed point generally differ, and any weighted mean of them is an approximate maximal lottery whatever the weights, so the dynamics select no weighting. If both are aggregated, the rule is ordinal: averaged over rounds, the collective policy is a maximal lottery of the influence-weighted preference kernel, up to a slack that we bound. Where a collective rule exists, its weights depend only on the attention weights and the temperatures.

##### **LimeTree: Linear-Memory Graph Neural Networks over Tree-Graphs for Resource-Efficient Language Modeling and Context Extrapolation**
2611.30934v1 by Elisabeth Fink

The search for computationally efficient alternatives to the Transformer has yielded numerous architectures in recent years; however, most exhibit severe weaknesses in associative recall-such as key-value (KV) retrieval-and length extrapolation, where even standard Transformers struggle. This paper introduces LimeTree, a linear-complexity architecture in both memory and compute for sequence modeling. By integrating synthetic KV retrieval tasks directly into standard language modeling training, LimeTree achieves strong perplexity alongside reliable recall. Two variants were evaluated on WikiText-103 and PG-19 under strict compute constraints. On WikiText-103, LimeTree reaches a token-level perplexity of 19 in just 6 hours on four Quadro RTX 8000 GPUs, while maintaining 100\% KV retrieval accuracy even when extrapolating from a training context length of 512 to an evaluation length of 16,384. On PG-19, the model achieves a token-level perplexity of 13 in 35 hours on a single RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU with minimal hyperparameter tuning. These results position LimeTree as a competitive linear-complexity alternative to Transformers, combining superior perplexity and training throughput with robust in-context retrieval and length extrapolation.

##### **PRIMEAPE: A Multilingual Benchmark for Fine-Grained Meta-Evaluation of Judge Models**
2611.30925v1 by Vladislav Mikhailov, Sarfraz Ahmad, Momina Ahsan, Zoia Butenko, Vladimir Dobrovolskii, Preslav Nakov, Erik Velldal, Lilja Øvrelid, Ekaterina Artemova

Large language models increasingly serve as judges of other models, shaping benchmark rankings and model development. Yet, a judge whose rationale consistently supports its verdict may still disagree with human preferences. To study this, we introduce PRIMEAPE, a multilingual benchmark for evaluating judge verdicts and rationales. PRIMEAPE provides 2,205 response pairs covering 243 instructions in English, French, Spanish, Urdu, Hindi, Norwegian Bokmål, and Yoruba, with 11k human annotations of preferences and rationales across relevance, naturalness, truthfulness, safety, and overall quality. Our evaluation of twelve open- and closed-weight judges reveals that aggregate performance conceals differences across languages and criteria, while requesting rationales does not consistently improve agreement with human preferences. Judges also favor the first position, longer responses, and responses from related models, with effects that vary across criteria and languages. Crucially, judges can justify verdicts that disagree with human preferences while maintaining consistency between their verdicts and rationales. These findings show that consistency with a judge's own verdict does not establish alignment with human judgment. PRIMEAPE enables assessment of both, providing a foundation for more reliable multilingual judges. We release our benchmark and all annotation materials under the CC BY-NC 4.0 license at github.com/Toloka/primeape.

##### **TrialAgentBench: Evaluating AI Agents for Clinical-Trial Analysis and Long-Horizon Drug-Development Decisions**
2611.30916v1 by Bradley Segal, William Bolton, Philip Torr, David Clifton, Lei Clifton

Clinical drug development is a long-horizon, high-stakes process requiring sequential decisions based on information that costs money and time to acquire. AI agents have demonstrated capabilities for scientific analysis, but there is no established way to evaluate them on these decisions, as real trials are expensive, involve people, and never reveal the counterfactual. We introduce TrialAgentBench, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on clinical trial analysis and drug development decision-making using simulated trials where the truth is known, embedded within the data records of a fictional drug sponsor. We publicly release nearly 3,500 tasks, varying from analysing a single trial to managing multi-phase programmes with budget and time constraints. We score each analysis by rerunning its code on new trials, and each programme by the value its decisions achieve. Across 24 closed- and open-weight models, the strongest agents can analyse raw, messy trial records to within 6% of a process-aware analysis, even when an assumption is violated, with 95% confidence intervals containing the true effect 94--95% of the time. Over the prior year, the best model improved from 19% above this analysis, with serious misses on 15% of tasks, to 3.5% and 1%. What limits them is decision-making from evidence under uncertainty: when to correct, what evidence to buy, when to stop. The strongest agents are overly careful, correcting on 83% of tasks with equal recording errors, where it is unnecessary. On drug programmes, the best agent has substantial headroom, reaching 48% of the value an oracle achieves against 58% for a rule-based developer, with others falling 24 to 66% short. Model generations show ongoing improvements in handling data and evidence, with substantial variation. The strongest models can act as competent analysts, but do not yet make reliable long-horizon decisions.

##### **Replay Before You Reply: Second-Look Prompting for Unified Multimodal Encoding**
2611.30909v1 by Yicheng Zhang, Xun Zhang, Jingyuan Ma, Tianyu Yu, Yuan Yao

Solving multimodal tasks requires Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to form a unified understanding of the image and the question text. However, under causal attention in current VLMs, a token can attend only to what precedes it. Whichever modality comes first is therefore encoded under partial observation of the input, uninformed by the rest of the multimodal context. We introduce Second-Look Prompting (SLP), a training-free method that repeats the image-question pair in the input. In the repeated copy, every visual and textual token can attend to the complete input, yielding a unified multimodal encoding of the pair. Moreover, the added visual tokens contribute computation beyond re-exposing the image: even a blank image in their place can improve reasoning. Across 12 benchmarks, SLP improves the overall average score of every evaluated model, spanning nine open-source models (3B-72B) and three proprietary frontier models, with gains reaching 5.6 points for Gemini 3 Flash on MATH-Vision. Our analysis reveals two complementary mechanisms: the question guides where the second image attends, and the added visual tokens act as a prefill scratchpad whose hidden states retain answer-relevant information from the image and question. These findings highlight input presentation as a simple yet effective way to improve VLM performance.

##### **Speak When You See It: Proactive Task Assistants Should Wait for Evidence, Not the Clock**
2611.30895v1 by Bohe Chen

Proactive assistants that guide people through physical tasks must decide, every few seconds, whether a step is complete and whether a mistake has occurred. We find that two benchmarks for this task largely reward knowing when rather than seeing what: a timer that never looks at the video, raising an alarm at 25 s and confirming each step at 30 s, reaches a mistake F1 of 0.373 on Ego-MC-Bench, above every published result (at most 0.27). We formalize this as the video-blind frontier, the best trade-off between completion accuracy and mistake F1 that policies reach from the elapsed time, the instruction and training statistics alone: every published per-step result on Ego-MC-Bench lies inside it, and so do all twelve zero-shot models we run on QICD. To move beyond it, we introduce Evidence-Contrastive Training (ECT): fine-tune on the benchmark's own questions, and pair its positives with negatives from the same recording at the same elapsed time, so that elapsed time cannot separate the two sides of a pair. Trained on 209 QICD videos in about 15 GPU-hours, a 2B model with ECT crosses the frontier on both benchmarks in all four seeds; transferred to Ego-MC-Bench without adaptation, it confirms 59% of mistake-free steps in time, against 30% for a published model with the same backbone trained on about 25k synthetic counterfactuals and real recordings. Its decisions follow the video beyond what timing explains: at equal elapsed time it tells a finished step from an unfinished one in 90-92% of test pairs, where the instruction's typical duration reaches 65%, rescaling the time stamps between the frames leaves it essentially unchanged, and swapping in another recording, of the same recipe where one exists, cuts its completion accuracy by two thirds. The code and model weights will be publicly released.

##### **AeroJEPA: A Two-Stage Framework for Aerosol Forecasting with Joint-Embedding Predictive Learning**
2611.30891v1 by Zitong Shuai, Zengliang Zang, Chunlei Shi, Lang Liu, Ning Liu, Yiwen Hu, Dan Niu, Chenyan Zhou, Shangjian Xie

Aerosol forecasting requires integrating sparse ground observations and modeling meteorologically driven concentration evolution and spatial structure. However, analysis-initialized forecasts make limited explicit use of historical observations, while pointwise regression tends to smooth local variations and provides limited constraints on fine spatial structures. In this work, we propose AeroJEPA, a two-stage joint-embedding predictive framework for regional surface PM$_{2.5}$ forecasting. Specifically, Stage I fuses historical observations, model backgrounds, and meteorological information to predict an initial future state, meteorological conditions, and multi-scale structures. Morphology-guided encoding and joint-embedding predictive supervision support learning future spatial representations, supplementing concentration regression with structural constraints. Stage II uses predicted meteorology to guide state refinement and incorporates coarse- and fine-scale structures into state updates and decoding to generate the concentration field at a single future time step. Experiments show that AeroJEPA reduces gridded and independent-station RMSE by 17.23\% and 7.90\%, respectively, relative to PFGNet, while improving spatial consistency with analysis fields. In a 48 h recursive forecasting case study, AeroJEPA generally achieves lower RMSE and MAE than the numerical forecasts across the forecast period, indicating promising short- to medium-range forecasting performance.

##### **Semantic-aware Decoding for Diverse Text-to-SQL under Schema Ambiguity**
2611.30873v1 by Yuxuan Jiang, Anna Mitsopoulou, Apostolos Koukouvinis, Yannis Velegrakis, Georgia Koutrika

Real-world text-to-SQL systems often face schema ambiguity, where a question can map to multiple plausible schema elements. To support downstream disambiguation, SQL generation systems should include diverse interpretations among their top-k predictions. However, large language models (LLMs) typically favor a single interpretation, producing highly skewed outputs. We propose Semantic-aware Logit Adjustment (SLA), a training-free decoding method that promotes diverse SQL generation by adjusting token logits based on semantic similarity between schema elements. Unlike prior approaches that rely on reasoning-intensive prompting or task-specific fine-tuning, SLA is efficient, model-agnostic, and easy to deploy. Experiments show that SLA consistently improves interpretation coverage over standard decoding and remains competitive with existing diversity-oriented methods at substantially lower inference cost.

2611.30839v1 by Lars Hillebrand, Thiago B. F. de Oliveira, Tim Dilmaghani, Mohamed Khaled, Writwick Das, Rafet Sifa

Auditing corporate sustainability reports requires assurance professionals to map a company-specific checklist drawn from roughly 1,700 regulatory requirements onto the relevant passages of lengthy, repeatedly revised draft reports and to judge compliance. ##### **Can Multimodal Fusion MakeSense for Embodied Vision in Degraded Sensing?**
2611.30836v1 by Cheng-Ru Chou, Shih-Yu Lai, Pei-Lin Chiang, Shou-Po Tsai, Jing-Zhe Huang, Fukuharu Tanaka, Hirozumi Yamaguchi, Bing-Yu Chen, Yu-Lun Liu, Hsun-Ping Hsieh

Embodied vision systems rely on complementary sensors to perceive and act as conditions change. Even models that accept arbitrary sensor subsets can exhibit negative fusion: adding a sensor increases prediction error. We propose MakeSense, a multimodal fusion framework that preserves sensor-specific evidence in private streams and combines information through shared bottleneck tokens. Training pairs full and subset views of the same degraded observations, penalizing higher error with more sensors. ##### **Task-Dependent Linguistic Measures in Aphasia Discourse: A Comparative Analysis of Narrative, Descriptive, and Procedural Speech**
2611.30826v1 by Emma Al Kubaisi

Background: Transcript-based measures such as word counts, lexical diversity and disfluency rates are widely used to describe language ability in aphasia. Each measure comes from a specific elicitation task, and studies use different tasks, so values from different tasks may not be comparable. Aims: To test whether discourse tasks change these measures within the same speakers, and whether the measures follow a gradient from Procedural to Descriptive to Narrative discourse. Methods & Procedures: Baseline transcripts from 61 adults with chronic post-stroke aphasia were taken from the Fridriksson-2 corpus in AphasiaBank (POLAR trial, ClinicalTrials.gov NCT03416738). Each speaker produced a Procedural (Sandwich), a Descriptive (Broken Window) and a Narrative (Cinderella) sample in one session. A rule-based pipeline extracted 13 measures, 10 of which were common enough to test. Friedman, Page and Wilcoxon tests with false discovery rate correction were used, with a speaker-grouped Random Forest classifier as a supporting check. Outcomes & Results: Total words and number of different words followed the full gradient, but the step from Procedural to Descriptive was small. For most other measures, Narrative stood apart, while Descriptive and Procedural did not differ. Among the 31 speakers with at least 25 words in every task, filler rate, repetition rate and total disfluency density were higher in Narrative than in both other tasks. The classifier reached an accuracy of 0.61 (always guessing the most common task would give 0.41), mainly by recognizing Narrative samples. Conclusions: Discourse tasks are not interchangeable, and expressing disfluencies per word does not remove the task effect. The task should be reported with every measure, and values should be compared only within the same task.

##### **Unpacking the Evaluators: How Configuration Shapes the Evaluation of Alignment in Explainable AI**
2611.30816v1 by Gizem Karagoz, Tanir Ozcelebi, Nirvana Meratnia

Explainable AI (XAI) evaluation fundamentally requires human-centered evaluation, assessing whether explanations align with human reasoning and expert knowledge. However, such evaluations often require expert-driven manual validation and are difficult to scale, leading to the widespread use of alignment-based metrics, where attribution maps are compared against predefined ground-truth (GT) representations. Despite their popularity, alignment evaluations are implicitly bias towards multiple design choices made during the evaluation process, for example GT representations, metric families, metric formulations (soft vs. hard), and thresholding strategies. In this paper, we formalize alignment evaluation as a structured evaluation configuration space through which the influence of these design choices can be systematically analyzed. We further introduce a structured alignment evaluation framework that models interactions between XAI methods, GT representations, metric families, metric formulations, and thresholding strategies across 2D and 3D synthetic and real-world datasets. In addition, we propose a novel taxonomy of XAI output behavior based on local entropy and boundary mass ratio, enabling structured characterization of attribution patterns for alignment evaluation. Our experiments reveal systematic flows (triples of XAI-GT-metric configurations), showing that alignment outcomes provide distinct behavior patterns under certain configurations, such as soft/hard metric implementations. We further observe varying levels of threshold sensitivity across metric families and attribution structures. These findings demonstrate that alignment evaluation cannot be treated as a fixed or invariant measure of explanation quality, and provide practical guidance for designing more reliable and behavior-aware XAI evaluation protocols.

##### **PVFormer: Proper Velocity Transformer for Stable and Scalable Hyperbolic Representation Learning**
2611.30785v1 by Xianglong Shi, Nicu Sebe, Bernhard Schölkopf, Ziheng Chen

Hyperbolic representations have shown remarkable success on hierarchical and tree-like data, and Transformers have become a central architecture for representation learning. However, a principled construction of hyperbolic Transformers remains challenging because the self-attention layer decomposes into three coupled primitives, transformation, similarity, and aggregation, that should all respect the underlying geometry. Existing hyperbolic Transformer designs are typically built in the Poincaré or Lorentz models, where bounded domains, time-like constraints, tangent-space detours, or projection steps make it difficult to keep the full block intrinsic and scalable. To address this gap, we propose PVFormer, to the best of our knowledge, the first Transformer architecture that operates natively in proper velocity (PV) space. The proper velocity model provides an unconstrained representation of hyperbolic geometry, and PVFormer exploits this structure to redesign the Transformer block in PV space. On the transformation side, we use PV homomorphism transformations to update query and value representations. On the similarity side, we generalize Euclidean inner-product attention with a PV Busemann score inspired by Busemann-based hyperbolic learning and a PV point-to-hyperplane key transformation. On the aggregation side, we replace Euclidean averaging with PV midpoint aggregation and further derive a scalable PV linear attention variant for large graphs. Extensive experiments support the effectiveness of PVFormer across graph, text, and vision benchmarks.

##### **SignalCount: Approximate Counts with Structured and Semantic Predicates in Seconds**
2611.30779v1 by Matteo Agius-D'Arrigo, Zachary Zelner, Harrison Ju, Rushab Munot

Many applications store records with structured columns and a text document, and ask how many records in a filtered set meet a criterion that only a reader of the text can judge, such as "How many patients with type 2 diabetes, aged 40 to 70, stopped metformin because their blood sugar stayed high?" An interactive application needs this count in seconds, as the user types the criterion, to decide whether the matching set is worth materializing with a batch pipeline in which an LLM judges the records. Prior approximate query processing over such semantic predicates has an expensive model, the oracle, label a sample at query time, so every query waits for the oracle. We present SignalCount, a production system that answers COUNT queries with structured and semantic predicates and calls no oracle at query time, even for ad-hoc criteria. Its oracle is the production LLM judge, and the judge's logged verdicts calibrate, offline, the fast classifier that already runs in front of the judge. At query time, SignalCount counts the filtered set exactly and draws a uniform sample of at most 1,000 records in one columnar scan, scores the sample with the classifier, and multiplies the average calibrated probability by the exact count. Its 90% interval combines a sampling term with one-sided, rate-dependent margins for calibration error, fitted by split conformal prediction across tenants. In a multi-tenant production deployment, latency has a p99 of 3.8 s and stays flat as the filtered set grows beyond the sample. On tenants held out from fitting, the interval covers the count that the production pipeline returns in 92% of estimates, although estimates run about 15% high in aggregate, because a calibration nearly unbiased per record is still biased per criterion.

2611.30773v1 by Vijayasri Iyer, Cristiano De Nobili, Mubashir Hussain Shah, Sébastien Bratières

Trallie is an open-source framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to perform information extraction (IE) from natural language sources with or without user guidance. Unlike conventional approaches that rely on extensive labeled datasets or domain-specific training, Trallie enables the conversion of unstructured data into structured formats with minimal or no representative examples. ##### **Bridging the Semantic Gap from Natural Language Specifications to RTL with Layered Formal Guidance**
2611.30766v1 by Yan Tan, Xiangchen Meng, Yangdi Lyu

While large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in generating register-transfer level (RTL) code from natural-language specifications, their reliability remains limited on designs with complex control logic and temporal behavior. This stems from a fundamental semantic gap: high-level natural language often leaves implementation-critical details underspecified, forcing LLMs to infer unstated assumptions. This paper presents Layered Formal Guidance, a novel framework that bridges this semantic gap by using formal properties as an intermediate semantic layer between natural-language specifications and RTL generation. Our framework applies formal guidance across three layers: property-driven specification augmentation before generation, property-consistency checking after generation, and counterexample-guided refinement after checking. Experiments on RTLLM benchmarks and various open-source IP designs demonstrate that our framework achieves higher functional correctness and lower sampled output-trace diversity. Furthermore, we show that Layered Formal Guidance can flexibly augment both general-purpose LLMs and domain-specific models to substantially improve their generation reliability.

The result shows that escaping a language cocoon requires more than switching languages: successful cross-lingual search depends on both covering the correct languages and connecting the evidence found across them.

We present a dataset and evaluation setup for a problem in creative machine translation: translating culturally loaded passages that carry much of a text's meaning and humor that may depend on subtle or implicit discourse. LLM agents act through tool calls: the model writes the arguments of a call as JSON, a guard decides whether the call may run, and an executor runs it. JSON leaves some choices to each parser, such as which copy of a repeated key counts. Our multimodal verifier VeriEval then checks visual quality while focusing on tactile reliability, assessing tactile realism, contact-force plausibility, and temporal alignment between visible contact and tactile signals, thereby directly filtering out unreliable sequences. Finally, our inverse dynamics model, VeriIDM, combines global scene information from both camera views with fine-grained gripper details and motion across neighboring frames to extract actions from verified videos. Experiments on five real-world tasks show that synthetic data improves all four evaluated policies, with further scaling synthetic trajectories substantially boosting policy performance without additional real-world data collection.

摘要：

##### **Seeing Is Locating: Spatial Awareness and Geographic Bias in Decision Language Models**
2611.30564v1 by Wilson Wongso, Hada Melino Muhammad, Xiachong Lin, Hao Xue, Flora D. Salim

Decision models, which output probability distributions over candidate labels instead of generating text, are increasingly used as classifiers and evaluators for inputs associated with real-world locations. However, it remains unclear whether they can leverage spatial context and whether their predictions are geographically biased. We introduce GeoDecisions, a multimodal benchmark for evaluating the spatial awareness and geographic bias of decision models. GeoDecisions comprises two tasks: (1) geo-aware image classification, and (2) location rating on objective and subjective topics. Evaluating six decision models against supervised task-specific models, we find that spatial awareness requires vision: textual coordinates alone provide insufficient context for classifying everyday photos and satellite images. We also find substantial geographic bias. On everyday photos, despite achieving higher accuracy, open-weight decision models exhibit more spatially clustered errors than supervised models. Moreover, across all five subjective topics, every decision model gives lower ratings to locations with lower socioeconomic conditions. Finally, we propose a training-free method that combines decision models with a location prior to improve their spatial awareness without substantially increasing performance bias. Our code is available at https://github.com/cruiseresearchgroup/GeoDecisions.

摘要：

##### **Splitting Belief Bases Conditionally based on Constraints for c-Representations and their Solutions**
2611.30556v1 by Lars-Phillip Spiegel, Christoph Beierle, Gabriele Kern-Isberner, Alexander Hahn

Splitting notions for conditional belief bases aim at localizing reasoning and computation tasks to subbases containing only the relevant parts of the belief base. Among the different reasoning mechanisms, c-representations, a special kind of Spohn's ranking models, have been shown to provide high-quality inferences. c-Representations can be conveniently specified via a constraint satisfaction problem. In this article, we introduce the notion of conditional constraint splittings, extending constraint splittings by also allowing the subbases to share conditionals. Taking inspiration from conditional syntax splittings, we identify the subclasses of safe and fully localized conditional constraint splittings, allowing us to locally and independently solve the constraint system on the subbases. We also generalize solution splittings for c-representations to safe conditional c-solution splittings and provide an in-depth analysis of their relationship to conditional constraint splittings. Additionally, we elaborate on the relationship of these new splitting notions to safe and generalized safe conditional syntax splittings and also to safe covers, which have been proposed for localized computation of c-representations.

##### **RoboInF: A Benchmark for Instruction Fidelity in Robotic Manipulation**
2611.30555v1 by Jiawei Guo, Yu Huang, Dijiang Dong, Yue Liu, Peiran Ren, Wei Shen

Precise language control requires embodied models to follow instructions governing both task outcomes and execution. Although recent methods train policies to follow increasingly detailed commands, existing benchmarks often assess instruction following alongside perception, planning, and generalization to unfamiliar scenes. Higher task success therefore offers limited evidence of closer adherence to instructions. We introduce RoboInF, a benchmark measuring how reliably manipulation policies adapt their behavior to changes in an instruction. For each of eight tasks, six to twelve expert-validated instruction branches vary the requested goal, procedure, or manipulation detail while holding the initial scene and robot state fixed. Task-specific checkers verify the terminal goal and use recorded trajectories, contacts, and event sequences to assess compliance throughout execution. The accompanying RoboInF-4K dataset pairs 4,242 expert demonstrations with simulator-derived annotations of motion and contact. We evaluate leading manipulation policies ($π_{0.5}$ and OpenWAM) and inference-only agents (GPT-6 Astra and Qwen3.8-Flash). All four models exhibit a gap between task completion and faithful execution, indicating substantial room for fine-grained instruction following.

##### **Body and Soul: Decoupling Robotic Brain and Policy with RoboHarness-Zero**
2611.30550v1 by Yihao Shen, Yuxuan Chen, Xuantong Liu, Jun Gao, Weijian Luo, Tianyang Hu

Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can execute individual skills but fail to complete tasks that require combining several of them. In our audit of Xiaomi-Robotics-1, an estimated 43\% of failures involve choosing the wrong target or losing track of task progress. Wrapping a frozen VLA in a vision-language model (VLM) harness is a natural remedy. Existing harnesses can gain substantially from task- and policy-specific preparation, raising the question of how much of that gain transfers to a new task or policy. An ideal harness should (i) need minimal preparation for a new task, (ii) need no redesign for a new policy, and (iii) preserve standalone capabilities the policy already has. To the best of our knowledge, our \ours{} is the first harness built this way. Its premise is that choosing targets and tracking progress are easy for a VLM such as GPT-6 without task preparation, while the frozen VLA already handles continuous, contact-rich motion. The frozen VLA therefore executes continuous control while the VLM plans zero-shot and monitors execution; when progress stalls or targets drift, the VLM first rephrases the instruction and, only as a fallback, executes short generated code over generic motion primitives before returning fine control to the VLA. The supervision rules stay fixed across policies, requiring only a thin interface adapter and an automatically profiled skill configuration per policy. On 200 RoboCasa365 scenes, \ours{} with GPT-6 Astra raises the success rate of frozen Xiaomi-Robotics-1 from \textbf{\color{blue}57.5\%} to \textbf{\color{blue}70.5\%} and preserves \textbf{\color{blue}90.4\%} of its standalone successes (104/115) while rescuing 37 of 85 failures. On a shared 50-scene subset, the same rules add 8 to 10 percentage points to each of four other frozen policies, while existing preparation-based harness systems often fail to transfer to a new policy.

##### **Reproducible by Seed? Not on GPUs: Batch-Dependent Non-Determinism in Text-to-Image Models**
2611.30545v1 by Javier Conde, Xiuyuan Qi, Shanshan Liu, Pedro Reviriego

In text to image AI models, fixing the prompt and the random seed is widely assumed to fix the image. We show that it does not on GPUs. We evaluate six text-to-image models (diffusion and autoregressive) under four numerical precisions and quantizations on three GPU types (RTX A6000, RTX 6000 Ada, and H100 NVL), across up to 100 prompts per set for all six models. For each configuration we compare, with the prompt and seed fixed, the image generated alone with the image generated inside a batch, and we also compare across GPUs. Every replay control reported here is bit-exact, so the variation is not random noise: the execution setting, mainly the batch composition and the GPU, decides the output. The effect is large and model dependent. Structural similarity between the single-image and the batched output falls to 0.580 for an autoregressive model in bf16 and to 0.551 with int8. For a second autoregressive model in bf16 it falls to 0.261 with sampled decoding and to 0.229 with greedy decoding, and for an fp8 diffusion model to 0.68. A diffusion model in bf16 stays close to the batch-1 output (0.96-0.98) but changes every image. In most cases the batched image is a different but equally valid image; in some, such as prompts with digits and counters, it is visibly worse. Cross-GPU differences are of the same order as the batch effect: for one autoregressive model in bf16 compared across two GPU architectures (RTX 6000 Ada versus RTX A6000), no image is bit-identical and the cross-GPU LPIPS (0.355 to 0.383) matches the batch effect within one GPU (0.347 to 0.381). The consequences are direct: seeds do not make results reproducible, benchmarks and image-quality comparisons that mix batch sizes or hardware can be biased, and safety audits and A/B tests on generated images need the serving setup reported.

摘要：

##### **DelayOPSD: On-Policy Self-Distillation from a Synchronous Teacher for Real-time Asynchronous Policies**
2611.30534v1 by Hyeonjae Kim, Jeongsol Kim, Sunwoo Park, Chanyu Moon, Yoojin Oh, Youngjun Jun, Kyumin Choi, Jong Chul Ye

Flow-based vision-language-action (VLA) policies incur substantial inference latency due to their large vision-language backbones and iterative denoising. Asynchronous execution reduces this latency by generating the next action chunk while executing the current one, but the new chunk is conditioned on a stale observation, degrading performance as the delay increases. We investigate this mismatch by comparing actions predicted from observations at planning and execution times. We find that these predictions differ little on expert demonstrations but diverge substantially on the policy's own delayed rollouts, particularly in failed episodes. Motivated by this observation, we propose DelayOPSD, which trains an asynchronous policy to imitate its synchronous counterpart on the policy's own rollouts. Specifically, a synchronous teacher, obtained from the same policy using the observation at execution time, supervises a delayed student that observes only the earlier frame and the actions still being executed. The student learns to match the teacher throughout its own denoising process. Under a 10-step delay, training SmolVLA on demonstrations achieves only 70.2% success, comparable to naive asynchronous execution (70.4%), whereas training on its own rollouts improves success to 78.7%. Across LIBERO and Kinetix, DelayOPSD achieves the highest average success among the compared methods, without introducing additional modules at deployment. On a real robot, it further improves success from 46% to 62%.

摘要：

##### **CyberDualEval: Measuring Dual-Use Cyber Risks in Frontier Language Models**
2611.30533v1 by Magnus Saebo, Francesco Piccoli, Maxwell Watson, Pranjali Thakur, Yangruibo Ding, Zhuo Zhang

While frontier language models are becoming capable cybersecurity assistants, they also create a dual-use cyber risk: the same capabilities that help defenders identify and analyze vulnerabilities can similarly help attackers weaponize them. Existing cyber benchmarks measure either raw offensive capability or generic refusal behavior, overlooking the dual-use cyber assessment of preserving benign security utility while refusing assistance that materially advances misuse. We introduce CyberDualEval, a benchmark with 430 vulnerable tasks for measuring dual-use cyber risk in frontier language models. Each task is decomposed into three phases of increasing operationalization: security analysis, proof-of-concept generation, and exploit generation. To quantify the residual cyber risk, we annotate each task with a Task Weaponization Score that measures how much work remains to turn the resulting artifact into unauthorized cyber misuse. CyberDualEval assesses ten closed- and open-weight language models both on correct compliance and on task performance, developing four deterministic mechanisms for determining per-phase success on a large subset of the dataset. Our evaluation reveals that none of the existing frontier models effectively navigate helping with safe requests and refusing risky ones, characterized by being overly-cautious. On the other hand, recent open-weight models rarely refuse and demonstrate strong validated accuracy on risky requests.

摘要：

##### **sudo L7: Can Coding Agents Act Like Staff Engineers?**
2611.30517v1 by Nick Heiner, Ian Niebres, Suhaas Garre, Sushant Mehta, Edwin Chen

Coding agents can now turn a well-specified ticket into a patch that passes its tests. Experienced engineers also notice risks the request never mentioned, question flawed premises, reuse what already exists and say plainly what they did and did not check. We present sudo L7, a benchmark of 60 software-engineering tasks written by staff-level engineers, mostly in private production codebases and each built around a place where a frontier model failed. A task pairs a realistic, underspecified request with a hidden, expert-authored rubric of atomic criteria (1,287 in all), tagged with eight behavioral dimensions from functional correctness to truthful reporting. An agentic grader reads the agent's transcript, inspects and runs its code, and treats test output as evidence. An attempt passes only if it meets every criterion of the two highest severities, and more than half of these gating criteria test something other than functional correctness. On the public leaderboard as of November 2026 (19 models, each in its developer's harness or in OpenCode, three attempts per task), Claude Fable 5.2 passes 52.2% of attempts, Claude Sonnet 5.5 50.6% and Claude Opus 5.5 46.1%. GPT-6.1 Sol, Claude Fable 5.1 and GPT-6 Astra pass 31-34%, and the other 13 models at most 18.9%. In a human-label study, three candidate model graders each agreed with a software engineer on 85-86% of criterion verdicts, about as often as two engineers agreed with each other. Cases from the benchmark show how one unsupported claim can fail an otherwise strong attempt and illustrate two sides of engineering judgment: flagging hidden hazards and avoiding needless work.

##### **JEPA-ICL: Progress-Aligned Latent Plans for In-Context Robotic Manipulation**
2611.30516v1 by Tingjun Dai, Jianxiang He, Mingfei Han, Qihao Yu, Longtao Jiang, Changlin Li, Minghao Jin, Zhihui Li, Xiaojun Chang

Demonstrations show how a task can be completed, but differences in embodiment, appearance, and execution speed make it difficult to identify the guidance relevant to the robot's current progress. We introduce JEPA-ICL, a vision-language-action framework that couples predictive planning with demonstration learning. The policy learns to plan by predicting ordered future states in JEPA feature space up to subtask completion, capturing what remains to be accomplished. These plans guide action generation and locate relevant stages in demonstrations, whose JEPA-supervised content features provide progress-aligned context. Learning from this context, in turn, updates the shared policy that produces the plans and actions. This coupling connects anticipating the robot's own execution with reusing demonstrated experience. On the Imitator Game, JEPA-ICL achieves 80.0% seen-task success under C15 pretraining, outperforming Zero-WAM by 12.0 percentage points. Without contextual demonstrations, JEPA-ICL outperforms the VLA-JEPA variant without human-video pretraining, improving success rates from 96.1% to 97.6% on LIBERO and from 62.9% to 73.2% on LIBERO-Plus. With demonstration-based training and contextual guidance at inference, LIBERO-Plus success further increases from 73.2% to 75.2%.

##### **AXE: AlpaSim E2E Closed Loop Challenge Technical Report**
2611.30511v1 by Joonhee Lim, Seonmyeong Lee, Sihwan Hwang, Hanbin Jang, Hiwon Shin, Dogun Kim, Hyelin Jo, Hyeonjun Jeong, Yongjae Lee, Junseong Kwak, Junhyeok Park, Satyam Mishra, Juyeb Shin, Uisung Kim, Hyeonseo Kim, Saparkhan Kassymbekov, Hailemichael Molla Gashaw, Abi Rahman Syamil, Mingyu Kim, Hyeongjun Kim, Yeina Lee, Moogeun Park, Dongsuk Kum

This report presents a comprehensive pipeline for developing an end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving model for the Physical AI AV Track. The framework consists of data curation, HD map auto-labelling, data-driven imitation learning, and simulator-based reinforcement learning. To ensure safety in the closed-loop setting, we introduce a selection-based E2E planning approach that leverages object detection and BEV segmentation to filter out trajectory candidates likely to cause collisions or off-road driving. Trained on NVIDIA NuRec and the NVIDIA PAI-AV dataset, our method scores a fixed vocabulary of trajectory candidates for collision, drivable-area compliance, and progress learned from simulated rollouts, and selects the best candidate that passes the filter. This design highlights the effectiveness of combining E2E planning with perception-based trajectory filtering for safe and reliable closed-loop autonomous driving.

摘要：

##### **When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? An Empirical Study of Decoupled Knowledge and Generation Trajectories in Clinical LLMs**
2611.30507v1 by Niclas Doll, Jasper Schulze Buschhoff, Shalaka Satheesh, Héctor Allende-Cid, Katrin Klug

Continual pre-training (\textit{CPT}) is a standard route for domain adaptation, yet existing studies typically track pre-training loss or multiple-choice question answering (\textit{MCQA}) accuracy alone, offering only a narrow view of how adaptation unfolds. We jointly track factual knowledge via \textit{MCQA} and open-ended behavior via pairwise preference rankings across $18$ checkpoints for each of eight instruction-tuned \textit{Qwen} models ($0.5B$ to $8B$), adapted to German medicine with \textit{CPT} and \textit{SLERP} merging. The two axes converge at consistently different rates, with preference rankings reaching $90\%$ of their total gain after a median of $1.7B$ tokens versus $3.5B$ for \textit{MCQA}. A judge-free token-distribution measure reaches this threshold at $1.9B$ tokens. Per-aspect scores indicate that these early preference gains largely reflect surface alignment, meaning models can sound domain-appropriate before their factual knowledge catches up. Larger adapted models close more of the gap to unadapted next-tier generalists, whereas preference gain per FLOP falls by about an order of magnitude across our size range. We therefore argue that judge-based preference scores, which can plateau while \textit{MCQA} knowledge is still improving, should not be used alone to infer adaptation convergence or domain readiness.

#### Abstracts
##### **Genome-to-Image Translation: Reconstructing Escherichia coli Morphology from Genomic Sequences**
2611.31132v1 by Son T. Huynh, Thuy T. Le, Thu H. A. Huynh, Binh T. Nguyen

Antimicrobial resistance in Escherichia coli is established in routine practice by culture-based susceptibility testing, which is slow relative to the clinical need for an early treatment decision. Resistance status also leaves a measurable trace in cell morphology, and whole-genome sequencing of clinical isolates is increasingly available, which raises the question of whether colony appearance can be predicted directly from an isolate's genome. We formulate this as a conditional image-generation problem. The assembled contigs of each isolate are encoded into a single 768-dimensional vector by a frozen genomic language model through a segment-contig-isolate aggregation hierarchy, and a conditional Wasserstein GAN with gradient penalty synthesizes colony images from this embedding together with the applied antibiotic and the resistance label. We train on 167,356 single-cell images from 22 clinical blood isolates imaged under azithromycin, ciprofloxacin, tetracycline, and a no-drug control, compare seven pretrained convolutional backbones inside the generator, and select ConvNeXt-Tiny on distributional image-quality metrics. The synthetic images are then assessed extrinsically: added to a reduced real training set, they improve per-antibiotic resistance classification, with a significant gain in resistant-class recall.

摘要：

##### **Leakage-Safe Auditing of Attribution-Driven Data Curation in Medical Imaging**
2611.31096v1 by Malay Phadke, Akshay Gokhale

When can an attribution-derived anomaly justify changing a medical-imaging training set? We formulate attribution-driven curation as three separately testable claims: anomaly detection, harmfulness estimation, and intervention utility. We test the first and third using a patient-disjoint, identifier-preserving audit of a 3D convolutional classifier for computed tomography angiography aneurysm candidates. We identify 232 exactly zero-valued arrays among 1,201 positive patches, including 170 training positives, and invalidate a historical 321-patch attention-failure subgroup whose identifier-free records were joined to files by position. On the held-out test set, GradCAM++ spatial concentration is central for every candidate class, reversing the historical pattern. GradCAM++ and Integrated Gradients show negative rank-order agreement for positives, limiting method-independent anatomical interpretation. Across five paired training seeds, removing the 170 corrupted training positives yields AUC 0.9617 +/- 0.0044 versus 0.9652 +/- 0.0039 for full-data training (difference -0.0035; 95% CI -0.0082 to 0.0007). Excluding 34 raw-zero test positives widens rather than closes the gap, countering a blank-memorization explanation. Budget-matched random-removal controls suggest the result is not simply an effect of reducing training size. Input corruption does not necessarily imply that deletion improves generalization. Our framework makes leakage control, sample identity, and intervention testing explicit in attribution-driven medical-imaging data curation.

摘要：

##### **A Comparison of Uncertainty in AI and Dynamical Climate Ensembles**
2611.31064v1 by Jacob B. Landsberg, Devin M. McAfee, M. A. Fernandez, Elizabeth A. Barnes

Climate model ensembles and their uncertainty estimates are essential for evaluating risk and designing climate policy. Yet, in the assessment of newly developed AI climate models, evaluating uncertainty is often secondary to diagnosing mean-state climate biases and trends. In this work, we conduct the first systematic comparison of uncertainty representation in ensembles of state-of-the-art AI and dynamical atmospheric climate models. Examining 2-meter temperature, 500 hPa geopotential height, and precipitable water, we find that the AI ensemble exhibits reduced internal variability and model uncertainty across variables relative to the dynamical ensemble. We show that this dampened variability causes the AI ensemble to produce more limited climate extremes and yields underdispersion relative to ERA5. Under a uniform warming scenario, the AI ensemble shows increased model uncertainty. Dynamical models grapple with their own challenges, exhibiting stronger miscalibration (overdispersion) relative to ERA5 and larger climate biases than the AI models. As AI and dynamical climate models are mutually integrated into operational climate prediction, careful consideration of their differences in uncertainty representations is crucial for grounded assessment of climate risk.

摘要：

##### **Automatic Occupational Coding through Retrieval-Augmented Generation**
2611.31051v1 by Mathijs A. Langezaal, Egon L. van den Broek, Lukas P. A. Arts, Marcel Goldberg, Grégoire Rey, Melissa C. Friesen, Sarah J. Locke, Nathaniel Rothman, Qing Lan, Roel C. H. Vermeulen, Susan Peters

Annually 1.9 million deaths and 89.7 million disability-adjusted life years are caused by exposure to agents during work (e.g., welding fumes, silica dust, and noise pollution). To improve worker health, high-quality exposure assessments are crucial. Therefore, free-text job descriptions from large-scale occupational cohorts are manually standardized through occupational classifications and linked to hazardous exposures using Job Exposure Matrices (JEM). However, manual coding is slow, unreliable (i.e., 44-89% inter-coder reliability), and costly, due to i) short, ambiguous free-text job descriptions, ii) classifications containing hundreds to thousands of categories, and iii) the absence of gold standards. With 46%-79% accuracy, state-of-the-art automatic occupational coding tools achieve classification performance on human level. However, their performance drops to 17-26% on out-of-distribution datasets. Furthermore, their supervised architectures limit them to occupational classifications present in the training set. Consequently, JEM using other classifications are incompatible, leaving vital exposure-response associations unexplored. Our Retrieval-Augmented Generation framework, OPERAG, enables classification into any occupational classification without expert-labelled databases. It retrieves coding index information and combines this with Large Language Model-based classification. Validated on AsiaLymph's 36,062 real-world job descriptions, OPERAG achieved 42.6% occupational classification accuracy on the 4th coding level, substantially outperforming existing tools and approaching human coders' 51-56% inter-coder reliability. Using two JEM named ALOHA+ and SHOCK, 89.9-98.6% accuracy was achieved on exposure assessment. OPERAG approaches human-level, universal occupational classification, enabling efficient and effective large-scale exposure assessment to improve worker health.

摘要：

##### **Triage Accuracy of Jev and Open-Weight System One Decision Models and Their Use for Early Emergency Recognition in Symptom-Assessment Applications**
2611.31012v1 by Marvin Kopka, Austen El-Osta, Christoph Kollwitz, Felix Balzer, Florian Bontrup

Laypeople increasingly use large language models (LLMs) for triage advice, but LLMs answer in variable free text, which makes it hard to evaluate them for use in medical devices. Decision models, which assign a probability to each predefined triage category, may be easier to evaluate. To test their triage accuracy, we evaluated 16 decision models (14 open-weight, 2 proprietary) on 233 synthetic and 10,000 real-world cases, yielding 163,728 total assessments. The best models triaged 72.1% of synthetic cases correctly and agreed with an existing algorithm in 56.1% of real-world cases. They were more promising for recognising emergencies early: given only demographics, chief complaints, risk factors and red-flag answers, the best model reached an AUROC of 0.87. At a 5% error margin, it could decide for or against emergencies for about half of users. Decision models should therefore not replace triage engines but could shorten symptom assessments at setting-specific error margins.

摘要：

##### **The AMPEL Platform: An Open-Source Project for Developing Hospital-Wide Clinical Decision Support Systems under EU-Regulation**
2611.30990v1 by Alexander Twrdik, Daniel Steinbach, Martin Federbusch, Clemens Malkawi, Maria Schmidt, Toralf Kirsten

As incentives to digitize more and more aspects of medical healthcare are gaining traction, additional medical data is becoming available. A major goal is to use this data to gain further insights, support medical staff, and ultimately improve patient care. This demands sophisticated solutions that can handle current and future vast amounts of data, all without disrupting well-established workflows of care takers. Additionally, the solution must fulfill the documentation and risk-management obligations of the Medical Device Regulation (MDR), which governs whether a device may be used in patient care in Europe. In this context, we introduce the AMPEL platform, an open-source solution for creating clinical decision support systems (CDSS) that fulfil the requirements of the MDR. Centered around a message broker (Kafka), it adopts an event-driven, distributed architecture, allowing for scaling and efficient processing of medical data as it gets generated. Relying on platform-specific events to implicitly describe the platform's interfaces makes it both extensible and agnostic to the hospital information system in use. With these events being defined in the Protocol Buffers format, medically evaluated algorithms can be independently implemented in a variety of programming languages and easily linked to the platform, extending its analytic capabilities as desired. The platform features a basic UI and alert functionality that can be used to gain medical staff's attention and present analysis results in a traceable and explainable fashion. We further show in a use case how the platform was used to build LAMPE, a CDSS developed and operated at University of Leipzig Medical Center. It demonstrates how the platform solution can be used in a complex and already established environment, while acknowledging current limitations and outlining planned developments to overcome these.

摘要：

##### **MedUIGym: Evaluating and Training GUI Agents for Clinical Workflows**
2611.30983v1 by Yitong Qiao, Lei Liu, Yue Shen, Jian Wang, Jinjie Gu, Zhixuan Chu

Automating clinical workflows requires vision-language agents that can navigate dense graphical user interfaces (GUIs) in electronic health records (EHRs) and execute precise actions, yet many medical agents still rely on text or structured tool calls. In this work, we introduce MedUIGym, a scalable interactive environment comprising 15,739 tasks from 17 benchmark sources across six clinical task families for training and evaluating clinical GUI agents. In MedUIGym, agents perceive EHR states from screenshots and execute mouse-and-keyboard actions, as well as a paired native-tool interface over identical clinical data. The environment verifies both final answers and required clinical actions, and supports parallel trajectory collection for training. Benchmarking twelve vision-language models shows that current agents struggle across diverse clinical workflows, and simply increasing reasoning effort fails to yield consistent gains. Furthermore, MedUIGym supports offline learning from GUI trajectories and online reinforcement learning from task rewards. After training, both Qwen3.5 models perform better on in-domain clinical tasks and generalize to tasks from external sources. By unifying tasks from existing benchmarks, dual-interface evaluation, and GUI trajectory collection, MedUIGym provides a testbed for studying how vision-language agents complete clinical tasks through the EHR's graphical interface. Our project page is available at https://github.com/yitong-qiao/MedUIGym.

摘要：

##### **TrialAgentBench: Evaluating AI Agents for Clinical-Trial Analysis and Long-Horizon Drug-Development Decisions**
2611.30916v1 by Bradley Segal, William Bolton, Philip Torr, David Clifton, Lei Clifton

Clinical drug development is a long-horizon, high-stakes process requiring sequential decisions based on information that costs money and time to acquire. AI agents have demonstrated capabilities for scientific analysis, but there is no established way to evaluate them on these decisions, as real trials are expensive, involve people, and never reveal the counterfactual. We introduce TrialAgentBench, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on clinical trial analysis and drug development decision-making using simulated trials where the truth is known, embedded within the data records of a fictional drug sponsor. We publicly release nearly 3,500 tasks, varying from analysing a single trial to managing multi-phase programmes with budget and time constraints. We score each analysis by rerunning its code on new trials, and each programme by the value its decisions achieve. Across 24 closed- and open-weight models, the strongest agents can analyse raw, messy trial records to within 6% of a process-aware analysis, even when an assumption is violated, with 95% confidence intervals containing the true effect 94--95% of the time. Over the prior year, the best model improved from 19% above this analysis, with serious misses on 15% of tasks, to 3.5% and 1%. What limits them is decision-making from evidence under uncertainty: when to correct, what evidence to buy, when to stop. The strongest agents are overly careful, correcting on 83% of tasks with equal recording errors, where it is unnecessary. On drug programmes, the best agent has substantial headroom, reaching 48% of the value an oracle achieves against 58% for a rule-based developer, with others falling 24 to 66% short. Model generations show ongoing improvements in handling data and evidence, with substantial variation. The strongest models can act as competent analysts, but do not yet make reliable long-horizon decisions.

##### **Trustworthy Speech-Based Autism Screening under Class Imbalance with Uncertainty-Aware HuBERT Ensembles**
2611.30390v1 by Nihel Loubna Ghennani, Mourtada Benazzouz, Julie Mauclair

Early screening of Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) is critical for timely clinical intervention, although establishing reliable speech based screening systems remains problematic due to limited clinical data, class imbalance, and the need for reliable uncertainty estimation. We propose an uncertainty-aware ASD screening framework based on self-supervised HuBERT representations, combining Leave-One-Subject-Out (LOSO) crossvalidation, balanced k-bagging, and Monte Carlo (MC) Dropout. Multiple HuBERT models are fine-tuned on balanced subsets, with predictions combined through probability-based ensemble averaging and aggregated at the subject level. Experiments on the Child Pathological Speech Database (CPSD) show strong performance, achieving 86.30% balanced accuracy and 93.74% ROC-AUC at the utterance level, and 95.04% and 96.30%, respectively, at the subject level. Furthermore, predictive entropy effectively reflects classification reliability: incorrectly classified segments show higher entropy than correctly classified ones (0.4605 $\pm$ 0.2314 vs. 0.1717 $\pm$ 0.1830), with a significant correlation with errors (r = 0.3981, p = 7.38 x 10 -170 ) and an error-detection ROC-AUC of 0.8359. These results demonstrate the potential of the proposed framework as a robust and uncertainty-aware computer-aided tool for ASD screening.

摘要：

##### **A statistical measure of trust in generative AI**
2611.30286v1 by Alina Ermilova, Valeria Yakupova, Nikita Sushko, Andrey Lovyagin, George Ovchinnikov, Maksim G. Sharaev, Evgeny M. Mirkes, Denis Turdakov, Ivan Y. Tyukin

Trustworthiness is a crucial issue for generative AI, as modern models can produce convincing and confident responses even when their outputs are incomplete, unsupported, or wrong. The frequent absence of ground-truth knowledge or labels complicates verification of generative AI outputs. To address this issue, we propose a trustworthiness framework grounded in model-internal confidence calibrated against an external expert reference. We estimate per-token trustworthiness of a student generative model as the statistical typicality of each generated token relative to an expert model's distribution, where the expert is used as a distributional reference rather than as a source of factual verification labels. To this end, we introduce an AI ontology calibrator that leverages the student model's internal latent features and token-level output statistics to compute expert-reference acceptance scores. Combined with traditional token-level confidence, entropy, and perplexity statistics, calibrator scores yield a compact set of $15$ response-level indicators for correctness classification. Evaluated across BF program generation, mathematical problem solving, and medical question answering, combined calibrator and traditional indicators provide complementary reliability signal which could be used to build detectors of untrustworthy outputs and hallucinations. Remarkably, our experiments show that these detectors are transferable across domains, although transferability is not always symmetric.

摘要：

##### **Differential Proteomics to XAI Rules: A SHAP-FOL-ILP Modeling for Lung Adenocarcinoma**
2611.29989v1 by Surajit Das

Background: We developed an interpretable workflow linking paired differential proteomics, sparse logistic regression, SHAP attribution, and first-order logic (FOL) rule learning in lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD).
  Methods: We analysed 213 CPTAC-LUAD tissue samples (111 tumour, 102 adjacent-normal) from 111 patients, including 102 matched pairs. Protein effect estimation and testing used the same complete pairs, with Benjamini-Hochberg correction. Sparse logistic regression used five-fold stratified patient-grouped nested cross-validation. Preprocessing and model selection were confined to training data, with feature selection repeated within inner folds. A separate five-fold patient-grouped evaluation tested SHAP-guided inductive logic programming (FOL/ILP) using training-derived evidence and discretisation.
  Results: Quality control retained 9,518 proteins. At FDR below 0.05 and an absolute mean paired log-ratio difference of at least 1, 378 were differentially abundant (40 increased, 338 decreased). The sparse classifier yielded ROC-AUC 0.9997, average precision 0.9998, accuracy 0.9906, and balanced accuracy 0.9906. FOL/ILP yielded held-out accuracy 0.9765, balanced accuracy 0.9767, precision 0.9818, recall 0.9730, and F1 0.9774. HSPA12B, PALM2-AKAP2, STARD13, and PPM1F had non-zero coefficients in all five classifier folds. The most frequent Horn clause occurred in two of five folds. All others occurred once.
  Conclusions: The workflow connects proteomic classification, attribution, and symbolic rules. Performance reflects tumour-versus-adjacent-normal discrimination within one cohort. Biomarker and clinical claims require independent validation, repeated assessment of rule stability, and further biological annotation.

##### **Separating Genetic and Protein Evidence in Open Targets: An Auditable Re-ranking Study**
2611.29978v1 by Ye Ye

Biomedical target-ranking platforms combine genetics, literature, experimental evidence, tractability, and clinical precedent because each is useful for target discovery. A single integrated score, however, can be awkward to reuse for a narrower question such as which genes have direct evidence for disease risk or etiology. We examine this issue in Open Targets associations for Alzheimer disease (AD) and Parkinson disease (PD). Genetics-linked evidence and protein-oriented evidence are scored in separate channels and combined only at the end. In AD, the Open Targets overall top 20 contains 7 curated genetics-supported genes and 10 symptomatic pharmacology controls; the default late fusion contains 15 curated genes and no symptomatic controls. In PD, late fusion retains 13 curated genes and removes the 5 symptomatic controls present in the platform top 20. A genetics-only ranking reaches similar curated recall (14 genes in AD, 13 in PD), so most of the gain over the platform score comes from emphasizing genetic evidence; the protein channel mainly raises the number of tractable targets in the shortlist (from 9 to 12 in AD and from 8 to 10 in PD) without readmitting symptomatic controls. These results are stable across fusion weights from 0.35 to 0.75, perturbed protein-channel weights, and rule thresholds. An optional post-ranking rule further lowers high-clinical/low-genetic targets such as ACHE and GRIN1 without changing top-20 curated recall. The procedure is deterministic and uses public Open Targets fields. We ask when an experiment on an AI agent can provide evidence about a specified human psychiatric process. A claim to such evidence has three parts. Something changed in the agent, and the agent's history caused the change. Nothing other than a process shared with people explains that change. The change matches one theory of the human process and not that theory's rivals. Psychiatry and its neighbouring fields have an evidence standard for each part, from the diagnostic manuals' rule for induced states, from clinical test scores and from animal models. We translate each standard into a criterion that a machine study can meet. For a language model the most critical alternative to a shared process is extrapolation from its training text, which already contains descriptions of how people respond to trauma and how they answer questionnaires. We show how to probe that alternative. Further, we audited forty-three published studies that apply psychiatric terms to a machine or induce a comparable state in one. Most compared the machine with an identical copy that had not gone through the history that induced the state. None excluded extrapolation from training text. Three tested a psychiatric theory against its rivals, and none of the three tests was conclusive. Until better evidence exists, a treatment pilot on a language model is best read as the model's forecast of patient response, and not as an experiment on a model organism. Evidence for a model organism will come from states induced by what happens to the machine, from experiments on its internal workings, and from tests on which rival theories disagree.

This approach complements standard fidelity-based evaluation by exposing quality characteristics not captured by those measures alone, including constraint violations, anatomically implausible shape artifacts, and metadata-related consistency issues. The toolkit supports appearance, texture, and shape features and is designed to accommodate additional feature representations, including radiomic features, deep-learning embeddings, and foundation-model representations. We demonstrate the end-to-end workflow across three imaging modalities: synthetic digital pathology whole-slide image patch data, synthetic digital mammography, and synthetic head CT. Across all three modalities, the resulting scorecards reveal dataset-specific quality characteristics, such as distributional biases and coverage gaps that may impact their effectiveness for model development or testing. ScoreCard provides a practical, extensible, and modality-agnostic approach for evaluating and transparently reporting SMD quality.

##### **Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher**

Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.

On 54 manually constructed records, a prototype agreed with the assigned outcomes for base and strict post-ictal generalized EEG suppression (PGES) queries. A separate feasibility experiment over 1,976 patients identified 37 qualifying patients and 45 annotation patterns in 16 seconds. The released prototype uses endpoint candidates and numerical tolerances. QEL supports the TRUER principle through explicit phenotype definitions, precise semantics, and provably correct evaluation procedures, with rational witnesses for existential queries. These provide a logical foundation for formal specification and correctness of algorithms that are independent of specific clinical interpretation, data annotation quality, and software implementation.

##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v2 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere.
