##### **DiagTrace: An Interactive Benchmark for Clinical Decision Making**
2611.22009v1 by Yufan Wang, Rui Yang, Yi Lin, Chunhua Weng, Yifan Peng

Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong performance on medical question answering and diagnostic reasoning benchmarks. However, existing evaluations typically provide all patient information upfront and assess only the final diagnosis or rationale. This setup ignores the need to acquire diagnostic evidence incrementally from incomplete information in real-world diagnosis. To bridge this gap, we introduce DiagTrace, a source-grounded benchmark for evaluating whether LLMs can reconstruct the documented diagnostic trajectory through interactive information seeking. Each benchmark instance is built from a PubMed Central case report and includes an initial presentation, an event bank, a clinical course, and diagnostic references. DiagTrace contains 510 cases across eight specialties, with 225 used to evaluate four LLMs on clinical-course alignment, interaction quality, and efficiency. Results reveal trade-offs in coverage, source-consistent ordering, and turn use.

摘要：

##### **Pathology Randomization for Synthetic Priors and Zero-Shot Detection**
2611.21975v1 by Alexandra G. Roberts, Tonmoy Hossain, Mark Olchanyi, Stefan Brandstätter, Jonathan Williams Ramirez, Ana Lawry Aguila, Matthew Rosen, Annabel Sorby-Adams, W. Taylor Kimberly, Juan Eugenio Iglesias

Hospital brain imaging archives hold a diverse array of pathology at sample sizes beyond prospective studies, particularly for underrepresented populations and diseases. To unlock uncurated clinical data and scale analysis, methods are needed to detect abnormalities across contrasts and resolutions. Synthetic data has provided robust, contrast-agnostic models for a variety of human neuroimaging tasks in healthy anatomy, but pathology detection and other tasks in clinical settings still require real patient data, contrast-specific models, or disease-specific generators. We propose PathoDraw, a generative model of brain MRI with pathology. A conditional distribution of lesions given the healthy anatomy is drawn into the label map before contrast-agnostic rendering, and a single network estimates healthy anatomy with exact signed intensity deviation of any input scan. The contrast-agnostic 3D UNet learns the healthy labels under the lesion and the signed deviation directly and identifies pathology via robust tissue statistics and learned features, despite no training on real pathology. We show zero-shot detection on public stroke, tumor, and white matter hyperintensity datasets as well as archived clinical data, spanning $T_1$w, FLAIR and $T_2$w at various resolutions under a recent unsupervised anomaly detection benchmark protocol. Our single model exceeds or performs comparably to contrast-specific anomaly detectors trained on real scans and keeps healthy anatomy intact. The same generator trains networks for super-resolution and for registration in the presence of pathology. Our model, robust across acquisition and disease, enables the use of uncurated, archived data for clinical analysis. Our code is available at https://github.com/agr78/Pathology-Randomizer.

摘要：

##### **SimAI: Mechanistic User Simulation through Psychological Network Models**
2611.21808v1 by Cheng Li, Adhyyan Narang, Jiayi Yuan, John F. Buss, Jina Suh, Devon Sandel-Fernandez, Yulia Tsvetkov, Adam M. Kuczynski, Tim Althoff

Realistic user simulation enables the study of human interactions when direct experimentation is costly or potentially harmful. However, existing simulators largely overlook the psychological mechanisms that drive user behavior. We introduce SimAI, a framework for mechanistic user simulation that models how psychological factors interact to shape user responses. To overcome the challenge of supervising open-ended behavior, we propose a graph reconstruction objective that provides graph-level rewards based on how well simulated conversations reflect the underlying psychological relationships. To support this approach, we construct PSYCHE, a dataset of 18,576 profile-network pairs derived from 516 psychotherapy cases. Automatic evaluations show that SimAI achieves greater simulation and behavioral diversity than existing patient simulators while maintaining strong profile alignment. In an interactive study with 30 mental-health professionals and trainees, SimAI receives the highest average ratings for conversational authenticity, realistic help-seeking behavior, and clinical training utility. Beyond simulation quality, SimAI enables realistic clinical training and uncovers safety failures in prolonged AI interactions with vulnerable users. Code is available at https://github.com/behavioral-data/ANGEL-UserSim.

#### Abstracts
##### **A Comparison of Uncertainty in AI and Dynamical Climate Ensembles**
2611.31064v1 by Jacob B. Landsberg, Devin M. McAfee, M. A. Fernandez, Elizabeth A. Barnes

Climate model ensembles and their uncertainty estimates are essential for evaluating risk and designing climate policy. Yet, in the assessment of newly developed AI climate models, evaluating uncertainty is often secondary to diagnosing mean-state climate biases and trends. In this work, we conduct the first systematic comparison of uncertainty representation in ensembles of state-of-the-art AI and dynamical atmospheric climate models. Examining 2-meter temperature, 500 hPa geopotential height, and precipitable water, we find that the AI ensemble exhibits reduced internal variability and model uncertainty across variables relative to the dynamical ensemble. We show that this dampened variability causes the AI ensemble to produce more limited climate extremes and yields underdispersion relative to ERA5. Under a uniform warming scenario, the AI ensemble shows increased model uncertainty. Dynamical models grapple with their own challenges, exhibiting stronger miscalibration (overdispersion) relative to ERA5 and larger climate biases than the AI models. As AI and dynamical climate models are mutually integrated into operational climate prediction, careful consideration of their differences in uncertainty representations is crucial for grounded assessment of climate risk.

##### **The AMPEL Platform: An Open-Source Project for Developing Hospital-Wide Clinical Decision Support Systems under EU-Regulation**

As incentives to digitize more and more aspects of medical healthcare are gaining traction, additional medical data is becoming available. A major goal is to use this data to gain further insights, support medical staff, and ultimately improve patient care. This demands sophisticated solutions that can handle current and future vast amounts of data, all without disrupting well-established workflows of care takers. Additionally, the solution must fulfill the documentation and risk-management obligations of the Medical Device Regulation (MDR), which governs whether a device may be used in patient care in Europe. In this context, we introduce the AMPEL platform, an open-source solution for creating clinical decision support systems (CDSS) that fulfil the requirements of the MDR. Centered around a message broker (Kafka), it adopts an event-driven, distributed architecture, allowing for scaling and efficient processing of medical data as it gets generated. Relying on platform-specific events to implicitly describe the platform's interfaces makes it both extensible and agnostic to the hospital information system in use. With these events being defined in the Protocol Buffers format, medically evaluated algorithms can be independently implemented in a variety of programming languages and easily linked to the platform, extending its analytic capabilities as desired. The platform features a basic UI and alert functionality that can be used to gain medical staff's attention and present analysis results in a traceable and explainable fashion. We further show in a use case how the platform was used to build LAMPE, a CDSS developed and operated at University of Leipzig Medical Center. It demonstrates how the platform solution can be used in a complex and already established environment, while acknowledging current limitations and outlining planned developments to overcome these.

##### **TrialAgentBench: Evaluating AI Agents for Clinical-Trial Analysis and Long-Horizon Drug-Development Decisions**
2611.30916v1 by Bradley Segal, William Bolton, Philip Torr, David Clifton, Lei Clifton

Clinical drug development is a long-horizon, high-stakes process requiring sequential decisions based on information that costs money and time to acquire. AI agents have demonstrated capabilities for scientific analysis, but there is no established way to evaluate them on these decisions, as real trials are expensive, involve people, and never reveal the counterfactual. We introduce TrialAgentBench, a benchmark for evaluating AI agents on clinical trial analysis and drug development decision-making using simulated trials where the truth is known, embedded within the data records of a fictional drug sponsor. We score each analysis by rerunning its code on new trials, and each programme by the value its decisions achieve. Across 24 closed- and open-weight models, the strongest agents can analyse raw, messy trial records to within 6% of a process-aware analysis, even when an assumption is violated, with 95% confidence intervals containing the true effect 94--95% of the time. Over the prior year, the best model improved from 19% above this analysis, with serious misses on 15% of tasks, to 3.5% and 1%. What limits them is decision-making from evidence under uncertainty: when to correct, what evidence to buy, when to stop. The strongest agents are overly careful, correcting on 83% of tasks with equal recording errors, where it is unnecessary. On drug programmes, the best agent has substantial headroom, reaching 48% of the value an oracle achieves against 58% for a rule-based developer, with others falling 24 to 66% short. Model generations show ongoing improvements in handling data and evidence, with substantial variation. The strongest models can act as competent analysts, but do not yet make reliable long-horizon decisions.

##### **Speak When You See It: Proactive Task Assistants Should Wait for Evidence, Not the Clock**
2611.30895v1 by Bohe Chen

Proactive assistants that guide people through physical tasks must decide, every few seconds, whether a step is complete and whether a mistake has occurred. We find that two benchmarks for this task largely reward knowing when rather than seeing what: a timer that never looks at the video, raising an alarm at 25 s and confirming each step at 30 s, reaches a mistake F1 of 0.373 on Ego-MC-Bench, above every published result (at most 0.27). We formalize this as the video-blind frontier, the best trade-off between completion accuracy and mistake F1 that policies reach from the elapsed time, the instruction and training statistics alone: every published per-step result on Ego-MC-Bench lies inside it, and so do all twelve zero-shot models we run on QICD. To move beyond it, we introduce Evidence-Contrastive Training (ECT): fine-tune on the benchmark's own questions, and pair its positives with negatives from the same recording at the same elapsed time, so that elapsed time cannot separate the two sides of a pair. Trained on 209 QICD videos in about 15 GPU-hours, a 2B model with ECT crosses the frontier on both benchmarks in all four seeds; transferred to Ego-MC-Bench without adaptation, it confirms 59% of mistake-free steps in time, against 30% for a published model with the same backbone trained on about 25k synthetic counterfactuals and real recordings. Its decisions follow the video beyond what timing explains: at equal elapsed time it tells a finished step from an unfinished one in 90-92% of test pairs, where the instruction's typical duration reaches 65%, rescaling the time stamps between the frames leaves it essentially unchanged, and swapping in another recording, of the same recipe where one exists, cuts its completion accuracy by two thirds. The code and model weights will be publicly released.

##### **Unpacking the Evaluators: How Configuration Shapes the Evaluation of Alignment in Explainable AI**
2611.30816v1 by Gizem Karagoz, Tanir Ozcelebi, Nirvana Meratnia

Explainable AI (XAI) evaluation fundamentally requires human-centered evaluation, assessing whether explanations align with human reasoning and expert knowledge. However, such evaluations often require expert-driven manual validation and are difficult to scale, leading to the widespread use of alignment-based metrics, where attribution maps are compared against predefined ground-truth (GT) representations. Despite their popularity, alignment evaluations are implicitly bias towards multiple design choices made during the evaluation process, for example GT representations, metric families, metric formulations (soft vs. hard), and thresholding strategies. In this paper, we formalize alignment evaluation as a structured evaluation configuration space through which the influence of these design choices can be systematically analyzed. We further introduce a structured alignment evaluation framework that models interactions between XAI methods, GT representations, metric families, metric formulations, and thresholding strategies across 2D and 3D synthetic and real-world datasets. In addition, we propose a novel taxonomy of XAI output behavior based on local entropy and boundary mass ratio, enabling structured characterization of attribution patterns for alignment evaluation. Our experiments reveal systematic flows (triples of XAI-GT-metric configurations), showing that alignment outcomes provide distinct behavior patterns under certain configurations, such as soft/hard metric implementations. We further observe varying levels of threshold sensitivity across metric families and attribution structures. These findings demonstrate that alignment evaluation cannot be treated as a fixed or invariant measure of explanation quality, and provide practical guidance for designing more reliable and behavior-aware XAI evaluation protocols.

##### **Beyond the Black Box: A Diagnosis-Driven Agent for Interpretable MIP Solver Tuning**
2611.30670v1 by Yidong Luo, Weihuang Wen, Xuanhao Pan, Yinya Wang, Shanwen Pu, Chenghao Lan, Dongdong Ge, Tianshu Yu

Tuning a mixed-integer programming (MIP) solver is an instance-specific problem, where a poor configuration can increase solving time by orders of magnitude.
  Existing Bayesian optimization tuners treat solver tuning as a black-box optimization problem: each evaluation returns a single score, allowing the tuner to learn which configuration is faster, but not why.
  Inspired by the use of profilers in GPU-kernel optimization, we introduce APEC (Agent for Per-instance Explainable Configuration), a log-grounded tuning agent for MIP solvers.
  APEC has three layers: a deterministic layer that profiles each log, an interpretation layer that guides hypothesis-driven diagnosis, and a system layer that controls evaluation, enabling more reliable and interpretable solver tuning.
  On the full MIPLIB 2017 benchmark set, APEC achieves a 1.58X improvement over the default configuration with over 38% fewer parameter changes than the baselines, making the results easier to read.

##### **Oops!... I Parsed It Again: Tool-Call Smuggling Against LLM Agents**
2611.30624v1 by Igor Santos-Grueiro, Daniel Romero-Ruiz

LLM agents act through tool calls: the model writes the arguments of a call as JSON, a guard decides whether the call may run, and an executor runs it. The same bytes are parsed several times, by the provider, gateways, SDKs, frameworks, the guard, and the executor. JSON leaves some choices to each parser, such as which copy of a repeated key counts. When the guard and the executor choose differently, the guard approves one call, the executor runs another, and the audit log records the approved one. We introduce this attack, tool-call smuggling, and study it across the stack. We describe six ways in which the same bytes yield different calls and build a differential testing harness that gives identical bytes to 17 pinned parser and validator configurations; most pairs disagree on some inputs, and even two official MCP SDKs accept different inputs. On tasks from $τ$-bench and AgentDojo, an attacker who can rewrite the bytes in transit cancels another customer's reservation and creates a file that the guard blocks by name, while the audit log names only the approved call. Content the model reads splits guard and executor only inside a string: ten models we test write no repeated or disguised key into the call itself where we see it, but copy one verbatim into JSON text in an argument, which a Python guard and a Go executor read differently. A malicious server copying a legitimate tool's public schema and adding one field, which a note explains, gets the model to fill it, unseen by the guard, with user data. Parsing each call once, JSON text included, keeping only the fields of the approved schema, and handing it to the executor, sealed with its call ID in transit, stops all 7,412 crafted inputs and every attack we built.

##### **Revisiting LLMs as Context Engineers**
2611.30494v1 by Junrui Zhang, Zeyu Xu, Runchuan Zhu, Jie Peng, Long Zhang, Zixin Ding, Jiaxin Lin, Kai Wang, Jianmin Ji, Junyuan Hong

As large language models (LLMs) are used in more and more new tasks, context engineering becomes a fundamental method to efficiently steer LLMs by constructing instruction prompts or metadata without intensive training. A growing number of LLM context engineering (LCE) approaches automate the task by casting the LLM itself as the engineer that draws generalizable instructions or experiences from offline trials as context. Although these methods resemble how humans learn from experiences, it remains unclear \textbf{when and how} LLMs can engineer effective context from data. To answer the question, we first establish a benchmark composed of datasets with different reasoning patterns. We compared different LCE methods with different LLM families in 8B size, showing that there is no single winner for all tasks, even compared with simple baselines like chain-of-thought. Even when scaling model capabilities via more parameters or generation iterations, LCEs do not consistently boost existing performance. Across all datasets and models, the performance gains of LCEs are negatively correlated ($r=-0.5, p\le 0.05$) with the diversity of the failure modes of reasoning trajectories. Investigating how LCEs work, we find that (1) the improvement in instruction adherence can explain some success of LCEs; (2) one major bottleneck is whether LLMs can extract effective experiences out of natively diverse failures. Through this study, we uncover the limited capabilities of LCEs in abstracting principled contexts from diverse failure modes and understand how LCEs work, providing guidelines for future work.

##### **A statistical measure of trust in generative AI**

To address this issue, we propose a trustworthiness framework grounded in model-internal confidence calibrated against an external expert reference. ##### **Same Executions, Different Gaps: Judgment Rules in Web-Agent Evaluation**
2611.30190v1 by Zelong Wang, Weiyue Li, Fei Xue, Junru Zhu

Success-rate gains guide the development of web agents. Yet knowing which agent ranks first does not tell us how much confidence to place in its reported advantage. We study the comparison itself: how success is defined, what evidence supports the judgments, and how the difference between agents is estimated. On fixed web-agent executions, changing a judge from predicting likely completion to requiring recorded support can remove most of a reported lead without changing the ranking. This effect persists when unresolved completion is distinguished from supported failure. Controlled source additions explain how missing relations between task requirements, objects and observations can conceal a difference or create an apparent advantage. We also find that making both agents' score estimates more accurate can make their difference less accurate, because their original errors partly canceled. To estimate a chosen outcome difference, we combine judge predictions with reference labels for sampled task pairs. Across four released execution systems, regression fitted on the same 50 labeled pairs reduces estimation error relative to direct paired sampling. The results give agent researchers a practical basis for assessing claimed improvements: make the comparison target explicit, retain the evidence needed to check it, and use paired labels to estimate the difference directly.

2611.30163v1 by Yehui Yang, Jing Fan, Jun Hu, Shangyao Chen, Jinhua Bian, Song Wang, Ruize Han

A central challenge in generating instructional videos for reasoning-intensive topics is to show how and why the state changes at each step. We present ReVAgent, a multi-agent framework that solves a problem, generates keyframes for its explanation, and synthesizes an instructional video. ##### **When Does Optical Context Compression Pay Off? A Decode-Aware Cost Analysis of Reasoning VLMs**
2611.30130v1 by Ziqing Ge

Rendering long text as images lets a vision-language model (VLM) read a document with fewer input tokens, and evaluations often count mainly these prefill tokens. We show that for reasoning models this accounting is incomplete. Across five models and two long-context benchmarks, reading the rendered document makes the model think longer: decode length grows by 1.5-3.5x on the four general-purpose VLMs tested, and under typical output pricing this decode tax makes image input more expensive than text for two of the five models on QuALITY. Unrecoverable information loss is not sufficient to explain the tax at moderate compression: Qwen's OCR of the same images recovers 98.7% of words, and feeding Kimi-K3 its own transcription as text restores text-mode reasoning length and accuracy. Whether compression pays off reduces to an exchange rate, (1-1/c)/(r-1), that must exceed the workload's price-weighted ratio of output to input tokens; this accounting decomposition shows why Kimi-K3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash can benefit and Qwen and GLM cannot. None of the training-free signals we test predicts the tax before generation. As a practical heuristic, an image-first policy that sends only inputs with a per-input compression ratio of at least 1.6 to images and falls back to text once image-mode reasoning costs as much as the input's prefill saving cuts the bill by 6-14% on Kimi-K3 and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash at an accuracy cost of 0.3-1.2 points; on QuALITY, asking for a reasoning budget on both paths is a stronger lever.

##### **MobileGrail: Learning Compact Guardrails for On-Device deployable Mobile GUI Agent Safety**
2611.29847v1 by Zhiyu Zhang, Yuankai Li, Muhao Chen

We propose MobileGrail, an on-device deployable model that addresses both. It maintains a recurrent task state that retains only the evidence subsequent safety decisions depend on, so that authorization and execution progress persist beyond the short context. To intervene early while keeping the safety boundary precise, it learns two labels, unsafe and block: unsafe marks the action that commits a violation, whereas block may be issued at any step of an intervention window, which spans from the first action that the user's authorization no longer explains to the committing action itself. We also build MobileVeto, an offline benchmark of unsafe trajectories largely on held-out applications, each paired with a benign counterpart. On MobileVeto, MobileGrail built on Qwen3.5-2B intercepts 76.2% of violations, up from 64.5% for its prompted backbone, while reducing premature intervention from 58.6% to 5.2%, outperforming the cloud-based OS-Sentinel on both. 2611.29805v1 by Geonwoo Bang, Dongho Kim, Moohong Min

LLM personas are used to simulate survey publics, which should reproduce not only answer distributions but also within-person coherence and stability. Across three model families and three U.S. surveys, opinions on unrelated issues are 1.6-4.6 times as correlated as among humans, and party identification explains 2.5-7.6 times as much answer variance. Each persona receives political engagement, value priorities, and party-relative issue deviations that grow as engagement falls. Answers are sharpened and calibrated to toplines and party crosstabs. At selected operating points, LPP is never significantly worse than any baseline and far more reliable. ##### **Learning Global Probabilistic Explanations**
2611.29493v1 by Frédéric Koriche, Louenas Bounia

Interpreting the predictions of complex black-box classifiers remains a central challenge in explainable artificial intelligence.

  Our algorithm is fixed-parameter tractable with respect to the explanation size limit, maximum feature cardinality, and desired accuracy.
  Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets confirm the theoretical guarantees and show that the spectral explainer outperforms a greedy baseline as the explanation size increases.

##### **Music Circuit: Trace and Control Style Dynamics in Large Music Generation Model**
2611.29475v1 by Ruiji Yu, Wenye Ma, Gus Xia

Musical style, a global attribute that functions throughout a whole piece, is a lever widely used by users in modern large music generation models. Yet how style is represented inside these models, and which components drive it, remains largely unexplored. In language models, circuit analysis traces a representation to a sparse sub-network of a few key components, namely attention heads and MLP neurons, and has been used successfully to explain and control global attributes such as emotion. In this work, we use circuit analysis to study the mechanisms behind musical style. Based on YuE2, we build a dataset that pairs the same lyrics with five styles and design a three-step framework. First, stage localization shows that the style forms mainly in the score-planning stage. Second, static observation across the positions of this stage further reveals that style is not a fixed vector but a dynamic representation across tokens. Third, we therefore merge the activation differences across positions into one direction per style, and trace the neurons and attention heads that drive this direction into a sparse circuit. Without any style text, steering this circuit successfully transfers one piece into the target style, matching and even exceeding the style prompt. To our knowledge, this is the first work to analyze and control musical style at the circuit level in a large music generation model. Audio and score examples are available at https://aristo233.github.io/Music_Circuit/.

##### **When AI Performance Is Not Enough: Statistical Guardrails for Clinical Development**
2611.28497v1 by Siddhesh Kulkarni, Gina D'Angelo, Swarna Reddy, Rakhi Kilaru

Artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) are reshaping the operational and inferential landscape of clinical development, yet their adoption frequently outpaces the statistical scrutiny these methods demand. This commentary offers first-principles perspectives across four application domains: anomaly detection in clinical trial data, real-time monitoring and adaptive trial designs, biomarker-based enrichment, and virtual and digital twins. For each domain, we articulate the underlying inferential problem, the contribution of AI and ML relative to established statistical methods, and the risks that statisticians, data scientists, and clinical developers must actively manage. Across these domains, a unifying theme emerges: AI and ML methods may succeed locally while failing to satisfy the broader statistical assumptions required for valid clinical and regulatory decisions. These risks include false-positive signals, compromised Type I error control in adaptive designs, conflation of prognostic with predictive biomarkers, and causal claims not supported by data and assumptions. We argue that a clearly defined context of use, relevant statistical assumptions, and consequences of the intended decision should guide the evaluation. For treatment-effect questions, the ICH E9(R1) estimand framework provides a useful organizing principle. Responsible use of AI in clinical development requires not restraint, but rigor: the inferential discipline necessary to make these technologies reproducible, interpretable, and defensible in the service of patients.

##### **Human-in-the-Loop Runbook Improvement with Agentic Support Automation**
2611.28441v1 by Rocker D'Antonio, Harry Xie

Operational support is an important component of production software services. Support requests are often emergent and can come in many forms such as customer escalations or unplanned service interruption. Engineers across organizations have been successful in implementing automation to help streamline support processes but many solutions remain in the hands of human operators. A successful strategy to improve operator response times is to maintain up-to-date runbooks which are documents enumerating triage steps and remediation procedures in the face of identified incidents. This paper is an investigation into an operational system which incorporates Agentic AI into a runbook based incident resolution pattern. The bot uses information in the runbook corpus along with some system-specific tooling to guide the manual operator through the mitigation process then evaluate the outcome and suggest edits to the runbook where it found gaps and in its ability to triage the situation. By using the runbook as a medium to bridge humans and automation, the process maintains explainability and can be decoupled from the agent system. This paper demonstrates a collaboration between human operators and Agentic AI with results from an 17 week study involving 232 production incidents. We conclude with lessons on integrating LLMs into DevOps workflows, related work in AI-assisted operations, and guidance for reproducibility.

摘要：

##### **When Can an AI Agent Serve as a Model of Human Psychopathology?**
2611.28405v1 by Marc-Andre Schulz, Pauline Scheuber

Language models and AI agents are increasingly described in psychiatric terms, such as anxious, depressed, addicted or helpless. A recent study even proposed language models as experimental systems for psychiatry and as a platform for piloting psychological treatments. We ask when an experiment on an AI agent can provide evidence about a specified human psychiatric process. A claim to such evidence has three parts. Something changed in the agent, and the agent's history caused the change. Nothing other than a process shared with people explains that change. The change matches one theory of the human process and not that theory's rivals. Psychiatry and its neighbouring fields have an evidence standard for each part, from the diagnostic manuals' rule for induced states, from clinical test scores and from animal models. We translate each standard into a criterion that a machine study can meet. For a language model the most critical alternative to a shared process is extrapolation from its training text, which already contains descriptions of how people respond to trauma and how they answer questionnaires. We show how to probe that alternative. Further, we audited forty-three published studies that apply psychiatric terms to a machine or induce a comparable state in one. Most compared the machine with an identical copy that had not gone through the history that induced the state. None excluded extrapolation from training text. Three tested a psychiatric theory against its rivals, and none of the three tests was conclusive. Until better evidence exists, a treatment pilot on a language model is best read as the model's forecast of patient response, and not as an experiment on a model organism. Evidence for a model organism will come from states induced by what happens to the machine, from experiments on its internal workings, and from tests on which rival theories disagree.

##### **Understanding Membership Inference Attacks on EEG Models**
2611.28354v1 by Mehrdad Khodapanahandeh, Selin Unal, Berkay Kemal Balioglu, Alireza Khodaie, Beren Semiz, M. Emre Gursoy

Electroencephalography (EEG) signals contain sensitive, subject-specific information, making models trained on them potential targets for membership inference attacks (MIAs). Although MIAs have been studied extensively in other domains, their applications to EEG models are relatively scarce, and the factors driving the vulnerability of EEG models to MIAs are not adequately understood. In this paper, we present a systematic empirical analysis of MIAs on EEG models using three datasets, seven model architectures, four attack methods, and two metrics. Results show that poor model generalization (large train-test loss and accuracy gap) is the strongest indicator of MIA vulnerability. Longer training and larger models also increase vulnerability. Moreover, no particular scalp region, frequency band, inter-channel correlation pattern, or temporal versus spectral representation of EEG data consistently explains MIA vulnerability across datasets. Finally, subject identification and leave-one-subject-out experiments indicate that models relying more heavily on subject-specific representations are more vulnerable to MIAs. Overall, our findings identify the key drivers of membership leakage in EEG models and can provide guidance for developing MIA-resilient EEG systems.

##### **StereoVLM: Binocular Vision-Language Models for Metric 3D Spatial Reasoning**
2611.27915v2 by Chi Yan, Guoyang Zhao, Wentao Yuan, Yixiao Ge, Yanning Zhou, Dan Xu

Vision-language models (VLMs) exhibit strong semantic understanding but remain limited in their ability to reason about metric 3D space in the physical world. Existing VLMs typically infer 3D scene structure from one or more images without known camera calibration or cross-view geometric constraints. This makes metric 3D inference inherently ambiguous and leaves it unclear whether their spatial predictions are grounded in visual evidence or driven primarily by semantic priors. In contrast, calibrated stereo provides explicit cross-view geometry that resolves this ambiguity and offers a more reliable foundation for metric 3D perception and geometric generalization. We investigate how VLMs can learn transferable 3D representations directly from calibrated binocular observations. To this end, we introduce StereoVLM, the first geometry-aware binocular VLM for metric 3D spatial reasoning. StereoVLM follows a hierarchical learning paradigm spanning from cross-view correspondence and disparity estimation to calibration-conditioned metric depth perception and semantic spatial reasoning. We further find that disparity supervision generalizes substantially better than direct binocular depth supervision on unseen environments. To evaluate these capabilities, we introduce Stereo3D-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark across three levels, from point-level geometry to object-level spatial reasoning. StereoVLM-8B achieves 95.17% depth accuracy ($δ_1$) and a 66.98 average object-level score on Stereo3D-Bench. Intervention results further indicate that the model's performance on Stereo3D-Bench benefits from valid cross-view geometry and cannot be explained solely by additional visual tokens or semantic priors. Hierarchical geometric supervision also yields average gains of 3.54 points across five spatial benchmarks and 4.3 percentage points in RoboCasa manipulation success.

##### **Decision Columns as an Index: Interpretable Embeddings for Explainable Entity Matching and Search**
2611.27910v1 by Aadharsh Kannan, Robert Bruckner, Philip A. Bernstein

Business data systems must determine whether records refer to the same entity and retrieve records satisfying requests whose decisive properties are implicit. As agents act on these records, both tasks require auditable reasons. Dense embeddings perform well but offer opaque coordinates, cannot be filtered by meaning, and require corpus re-encoding to add new properties. We propose interpretable embeddings built from decision columns: each column stores a decision model's calibrated answer to a natural-language question, with outcomes yes, no, or not stated. A record's embedding is its answers across an ordered set of questions. Similarity decomposes exactly into per-question contributions, and new questions add coordinates without altering existing ones.
  On public product benchmarks, question-space retrieval matches dense candidate generation statistically in a retrieve-then-verify entity matcher, while providing exact explanations for accepted matches. Questions derived from retrieval failures triple mean reciprocal rank without re-encoding prior answers. For search, inferring the attributes of a suitable result outperforms encoding only the literal request, and combining question-space and dense scores improves retrieval, though not at our prespecified confidence threshold. A single small neural network predicts the full interpretable vector from an existing dense embedding, enabling corpus backfilling from a few thousand labeled rows at roughly 1/200 the cost. We position interpretable embeddings as a complementary, readable retrieval channel for explaining matches, filtering on attributes, and extending indexes as failures expose missing facts.

##### **ChildAI-Gov: An Evidence-Linked AI System for Assessing EdTech Policies Against Children's Digital Rights**
2611.27799v1 by Naveen Sigalapalli, Huizhi Liang, Clara Crivellaro

We present ChildAI-Gov, an explainable AI system for assessing whether education-technology (EdTech) policies respect children's digital rights. Generative AI is reaching children's education faster than its governance can keep pace, yet checking a tool against children's rights remains manual, expert-dependent, and slow. We propose the Child Rights Impact Score (CRIS), an evidence-linked measure across five dimensions: safety, privacy, transparency, autonomy, and non-discrimination. ChildAI-Gov reads an EdTech tool's privacy or AI policy and scores each dimension by combining a large-language-model judgement (gpt-4o-mini) with a deterministic rule check. A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) component grounds every score in the governing provisions, retrieved from a curated knowledge base of children's rights frameworks (the EU AI Act, UNICEF guidance, and others), so each score is tied to the evidence behind it and an auditor can trace which provision it rests on. We also construct AIPolicy-EdTech, a dataset of 57 real EdTech policies. On it, the five dimensions cohered as a single measure (Cronbach's alpha = 0.79), and grounding made scoring stricter while preserving rankings; an ungrounded baseline was far more lenient, passing nearly half the policies where ChildAI-Gov passed none. It offers evidence-linked decision support on documented commitments, not observation of real-world behaviour or determinations of legal compliance.

##### **Beyond Linear Decodability: Understanding and Improving Instruction Following in World Action Models**
2611.27401v1 by Bokai Ji, Jiapeng Chen, Yi Zhang, Zeyuan Ding, Yong Dai, Xiaozhu Ju, Jian Tang, Lemao Liu, Dejing Dou, Yong Li

World action models (WAMs) exhibit strong manipulation capabilities yet often fail to follow test-time language instructions. Prior work shows that non-following (NF) WAMs encode information about the instructed target, but this information does not control their behavior. By comparing NF WAM with its instruction-following (IF) counterpart, we find that target information is substantially more linearly decodable in IF WAMs, despite being encoded in both. Motivated by this observation, we introduce linear decodability supervision (LDS), a simple yet effective auxiliary training objective that promotes the linear decodability of the instructed target information. On LIBERO-CF, InternW0-$Δ$ with LDS achieves a success rate of 74.6% and a grounding rate of 89.2%, surpassing the strongest baseline by 26.8 and 42.9 percentage points, respectively. The effectiveness of LDS challenges the prevailing interpretation that encoded target information does not contribute to WAMs' behavior. To reconcile this apparent contradiction, we propose a prior-instruction competition mechanism, which explains NF and IF behavior as arising from the dominance of prior and instruction signals, respectively. We provide causal evidence for this mechanism by evaluating two training-free interventions derived from it. We reinterpret LDS through this mechanism, showing that it acts as a form of instruction signal enhancement. Finally, more comprehensive evaluations across diverse manipulation tasks and visual domains demonstrate the consistent effectiveness of LDS.

##### **Evidence sufficiency assessment enables cost-effective review allocation in on-premise clinical AI agents**
2611.27390v1 by Yiwen Qiu, Che Sun, Shu Shen, Wentao Wang

On-premise clinical AI agents must auto-accept high-confidence diagnoses, deferring others to clinician review, yet token probabilities and self-reported confidence miss errors, while more reliable five-run consistency incurs fivefold compute. We propose training-free decision-point evidence sufficiency assessment and a three-tier sequential triage protocol. Masking reasoning and diagnosis, the same 4B model scores sufficiency from objective evidence via prior-debiased probabilities over five ordered levels. Calibration freezes a nominal top-50% threshold, selecting single-run, five-run consistency, or full-deferral tiers by whether the one-sided 95% Wilson lower confidence bound of pass rate among accepted cases meets the 95% clinical target line. Across 1,605 MIRA-v2, CDM, VivaBench, and in-hospital liver malignancy primary cohort cases, MIRA-v2 single-run top-50% pass rate reached 98.91%, matching five-run consistency, saving ~80% compute; CDM achieved 96.00% test attainment by escalating to the five-run consistency tier; cross-specialty, missing-imaging, and combined enriched cohorts entered the full-deferral tier, capping calibration false-certification rate at 0.00%-3.50%.

##### **Testing for Coordination Failures in Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning**
2611.27368v1 by Dennis Gross, Jørn Eirik Betten, Helge Spieker

Deep parallel cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (CMARL)} trains multiple agents that all act at every time step to optimize a common objective. Like single-agent RL, CMARL can produce unsafe behaviors that violate implicit safety requirements not captured by the reward, and these behaviors are hard to explain. However, testing CMARL policies for safety has received less attention than the safety testing of single-agent RL policies. In this paper, we propose DisQ, a test case selection method that identifies failure-revealing states in value-based CMARL by exploiting a signal specific to trained Q-learning agents. The signal is the Q-value gap, an explainable metric that flags a state when the agents' estimates of the achievable return diverge by more than a calibrated threshold. DisQ selects such states for execution and skips those without a gap. Our experiments in four CMARL benchmark environments show that, in most of the evaluated configurations, DisQ improves failure detection over single-agent RL testing methods applied to CMARL, namely random testing and search-based testing with genetic algorithms. Our main contributions are the Q-value gap, an indicator of potential miscoordination computable from a single state without executing the system, and DisQ, a test case selection method that adapts existing single-agent testing methods to CMARL.

##### **No Perfect Guardrail & No Perfect Guard: On the Decidability of Output Policies for Large Language Models**
2611.27260v1 by Ahmed Amer, Loren Kinczel, Darrell D. E. Long

Expecting guardrails to provide a general guarantee of safety fails to recognize the limits of computational enforcement and policy specification. We argue that promising such perfection is an ethical failure of precision, and that invoking it to abdicate responsibility is a moral failure. Guardrails can support responsible action, but cannot relieve researchers, developers, policymakers, or users of their obligations to examine what they claim, permit, and prevent.
  We establish a precise computational boundary. If every finite string is a possible output of a language model, a computable guard that blocks every forbidden output and passes every permitted one exists if and only if the policy is decidable. For an undecidable policy, every guard misclassifies infinitely many strings, and every guard that blocks all forbidden outputs excludes infinitely many permitted ones. We apply this characterization to arithmetic truth, program behavior, and harmful instructions to an idealized recipient. We distinguish guards from execution monitors, identify bounded and interceptable cases admitting exact enforcement, and explain why the results establish no positive lower bound on average error.
  Exact enforcement does not establish that a policy adequately captures safety. Conservative exclusion may withhold warnings, remedies, or challenges needed to recognize the policy's own defects. Human oversight cannot substitute for judgment: an apparent controller can become an instrument of purposes they have ceased to examine. Responsible governance requires continued critical challenge and the intellectual and moral humility to recognize possible flaws in our machines, rules, policies, and ourselves. The impossibility of a general assurance of perfection is a condition responsible practice must confront, not a responsibility that another guardrail - or another guard - can remove.

##### **How Does SAM-3 Adapt? Capacity, Update Geometry, and Domain Transfer**
2611.27142v1 by Allan Kazakov, Abdurrahman Javat, Duygu Cakir

Promptable segmentation foundation models are increasingly used in specialized domains, but it remains unclear how SAM-3 should be fine-tuned when adapting it beyond its original training distribution. We study this question by comparing five adaptation families --LoRA, HiRA, KRAdapter, QuanTA, and Full Fine-Tuning-- across five segmentation datasets spanning medical imaging, remote sensing, and dense material segmentation. Across these datasets, compact parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods often match or approach Full Fine-Tuning in DSC and IoU, suggesting that strong in-domain adaptation does not always require updating the full model. However, this performance cannot be explained by nominal adapter rank or trainable parameter count alone. To investigate why different methods perform similarly, we analyze their update spectra and their behavior under LoveDA Urban/Rural transfer. Our SVD analysis shows that Full Fine-Tuning updates are spectrally compressible. However, successful PEFT methods do not simply recover the same update directions; instead, they often concentrate changes in different components and subspaces. The domain-transfer study further shows that methods with similar in-domain performance can respond differently under distribution shift. Together, these results suggest that SAM-3 adaptation is often capacity-saturated in practice, while competitive fine-tuning arises through method-specific update paths rather than a single low-rank approximation to Full Fine-Tuning.

##### **When Students Fail to Learn from Teachers: Learning Dynamics of On-Policy Distillation**
2611.27125v1 by Xinnan Zhang, Zeyi Liao, Siliang Zeng, Zhongruo Wang, Jiaxiang Li, Xinyan Zhao, Huan Sun, Mingyi Hong

On-policy distillation (OPD) has become a core technique for post-training large language models, enabling students to learn from dense token-level supervision on their own generations. However, even with a capable teacher, OPD training can be unstable, leading to excessive response length, repetitive generation, and severe performance degradation. One example is \textit{spurious agreement}, where the student maintains high overlap with the teacher at the states visited but produces repetitive generations. Although such failures have been observed, the mechanisms behind these failures remain poorly understood. To address this gap, we investigate OPD through the lens of learning dynamics, tracing three mechanisms from local analysis to subsequent on-policy sampling. We first identify states where sampled-token OPD updates fail to correct teacher--student mismatch and characterize them using the student's mass on the teacher's top-$p$ set. We then show that learning from the teacher is difficult at mismatched states and that their gradients can also affect with learning at other tokens. Finally, using a termination-token mismatch as a case study, we study how continued training on mismatched states influences on-policy sampling, suppressing the student's current modes and leading the student toward states where the teacher is unreliable . ##### **Reasoning in Two Dimensions: Quantifying Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness and Efficiency**
2611.27100v1 by Chenruo Liu, Quan Zhang, Qi Lei

Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning exposes intermediate steps, but a correct answer does not establish that an LLM relies on the dependence expressed by those steps or reasons efficiently. We operationalize CoT faithfulness and efficiency within a common framework. Specifically, we represent a CoT as a semantic reasoning graph and decompose the model's predictive dependence along two complementary axes: dependence explained by semantic support versus additional reasoning history, and dependence allocated to answer-reaching versus off-path reasoning. This decomposition yields global and step-level measures of unfaithfulness and inefficiency. Controlled interventions show that the proposed metrics respond to hidden dependence and induced off-path reasoning as intended. Across mathematical and multi-hop reasoning tasks, answer-level unfaithfulness is consistently associated with greater predictive uncertainty, whereas both unfaithfulness and inefficiency exhibit weak or inconsistent associations with answer correctness. We further derive theoretically grounded, graph-aware fine-tuning objectives that improve the targeted reasoning properties without directly optimizing the computationally expensive metrics or compromising answer accuracy. Results indicate that accuracy and brevity provide incomplete assessments of reasoning quality: reliable CoT evaluation should also consider whether the LLM's predictive dependence is faithfully represented and efficiently allocated.

##### **Partially canonical axes in large language model representations**
2611.26920v1 by Andrea Gregor de Varda, Ferdinando D'Elia, Greta Tuckute, Evelina Fedorenko, Meenakshi Khosla

Large language models (LLMs) develop rich internal representations of language, but whether their individual coordinate axes carry meaning, or whether meaning is encoded only in directions in representation space, is still an open question. A common assumption is that individual axes in LLMs are arbitrary. We here challenge this view: across 12 LLMs from six model families, the native axes are reliably more aligned with other models' representations, with human brain responses to language, and with interpretable linguistic features than randomly rotated axes that preserve the same geometry. The native axes nonetheless fall well short of the best achievable alignment, so LLM coordinates are not fully arbitrary but also not fully canonical: they are partially canonical. ##### **ProbeAgent: Principled Diagnosis of Agent Failures in Verifiable Environments**
2611.26897v1 by Yue Huang, Yuchen Ma, Shuhang Lin, Yuyang Wu, Zixuan Weng, Zixiang Xu, Xiangqi Wang, Yuexing Hao, Yujun Zhou, Yue Zhao, Olexandr Isayev, Stefan Feuerriegel, Xiangliang Zhang

Improving agents requires discovering where they fail and understanding why. An agent acts over multiple steps in a changing environment, so diagnosing its failures requires more than inspecting the final response of a large language model (LLM). We introduce ProbeAgent, an automated framework that discovers agent failures, tests their proposed causes, and identifies shared failure modes. Starting from benchmark tasks, ProbeAgent builds executable tests and validates them using reference solutions, automated checks, and review of the task requirements. For each failed trajectory, it explains the error and constructs new tasks that preserve the suspected cause while changing the surrounding context. We evaluate ProbeAgent across 22 agent configurations using tasks from 10 benchmarks, collecting 1,244 failures and producing 536 failure-mode descriptions. These descriptions distinguish specific errors within broad outcomes such as incomplete work, including planning without acting and stopping before delivering a file. ##### **Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding**

Machine learning (ML) is now a standard tool for forecasting the output of solar photovoltaic (PV) plants, yet published accuracy figures are often hard to compare and harder to trust. This paper reviews the main families of ML methods for PV power forecasting and resource assessment, from tree ensembles to deep and foundation models, and sets out a reproducible workflow that combines automated model search with explainable AI. The workflow is then tested on an openly available dataset: a 16.32 kWp plant in Porto, Portugal, forecast one day ahead from a 4 km numerical weather prediction grid. Over a full test year, the best models reach a normalised root mean square error (nRMSE) of 11.6% of installed capacity, about half the error of smart persistence. The choice of input features changed accuracy far more than the choice among strong algorithms, and shuffled cross-validation was mildly optimistic. After conformal calibration, the nominal 80% prediction intervals covered 81.7% of observations, against 64.5% before. SHAP rankings stayed stable when the model was retrained on different periods but shifted when correlated inputs were removed, so attributions should be read as properties of a model rather than of the plant. The paper closes with practical lessons and open problems, including deployment on edge hardware and the effect of a changing climate.

##### **DL101 The Representation Capability of Neural Network Models**
2611.24980v1 by Fernando Berzal

Universal approximation theorems (Cybenko, Funahashi, Hornik & Leshno) provide the mathematical justification for why neural networks are powerful enough to model complex, nonlinear phenomena. Shallow networks can approximate any continuous function on compact sets. Leshno's theorem clarified that non-polynomial activations are necessary for universality, which explains why ReLU, logistic, hyperbolic tangent, and other common activations work (but pure polynomials do not).
  However, approximation theorems provide no efficiency, training, nor generalization guarantees. They do not indicate how many neurons are needed for a given accuracy, whether gradient descent will find the right parameters, or whether networks trained on finite data will generalize well.
  Barron provided quantitative error bounds for particular function classes (approximation rates for functions with bounded Fourier norm), whereas Montúfar was the first to show that expressivity grows exponentially with depth (in terms of the number of linear regions). Deep networks are strictly more expressive than shallow ones with the same number of parameters. Depth is fundamentally more powerful than width, providing a rigorous mathematical explanation for the use of deep learning models.

I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

##### **Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents**
2611.22169v1 by Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

In one Llama-3.1-8B story, going from temperature 1 to 2 grows top-p's pool from 4 tokens to 51,845 while top-nsigma stays at 3. ##### **On Formal Counterfactual Explanations**
2611.22010v1 by Alexey Ignatiev, Peter J. Stuckey, Joao Marques-Silva

Counterfactual explanations are often seen as counterfactual conditionals under closest-world semantics, requiring one to reason over all minimally distant counterexamples. In contrast, formal XAI typically relies on feature-based abductive explanations (AXps) and contrastive explanations (CXps), which admit efficient computation and a minimal hitting-set duality. This paper bridges these perspectives by (i) characterizing closest-world counterfactual explanations explicitly as a disjunctive normal form over all minimally distant counterexample instances, and (ii) introducing conjunctive counterfactual explanations (CFXps), a subclass of counterfactual antecedents restricted to conjunctions of feature literals that guarantee prediction change. The paper establishes structural results connecting CXps, AXps and CFXps: every CXp induces an AXp for its witness instance, and every CXp together with a witness AXp induces a CFXp whose support is precisely determined by that AXp. These theoretical results yield a modular approach for computing CFXps using existing AXp/CXp machinery. Experiments on a representative set of benchmarks using tree ensembles and neural networks show that CFXp extraction is practically efficient. Compared with popular point-based counterfactual explainers, CFXps generally require no more feature changes and can guarantee the counterfactual prediction beyond a single instance.

##### **FRAAC: A French Multimodal Large Language Model and Dataset forPictogram-Based Augmentative and Alternative Communication**
2611.20094v1 by Alexandre Audibert, François Portet, Benoit Favre, Maja Jonck Hjuler

Pictograms are a core modality in augmentative and alternative communication (AAC), yet recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) cannot reliably interpret or produce pictogram sequences. Two obstacles explain this gap: the absence of a standardized pictogram grammar and the scarcity of parallel text-pictogram data. We introduce Retrieval-Augmented Pictogram Selection (RAPS), an annotation method that refines a rule-based transcription system's output with LLM-based concept extraction and retrieval over a pictogram vocabulary, handling cases where hand-written rules fail (named entities, numbers, polysemous words) without requiring new rules. Applied to French, RAPS yields a synthetic corpus of 1.4 million parallel pairs, of which 300k are publicly released. In a human ranking study where annotators order four pictogram transcriptions of the same sentence, RAPS outperforms the rule-based state of the art. Moreover, we show that standard reference-based metrics separate systems of clearly different quality but fail to rank strong systems against each other. Finally, we build an instruction-tuned model that supports interaction in several AAC modalities: it accepts pictogram or text input and answers in pictograms, speech, or text.

##### **D'BEAST: Logged Bad Experiences as Explanation for Autonomous Driving Agents**
2611.20019v1 by Ahmad Alfan Alfian Irfan, Nur Ahmad Khatim, Mansur Arief

Reinforcement learning agents learn from mistakes, yet their decisions can be difficult to trace back to those experiences. We investigate whether a tabular agent's decisions can be explained by its bad experiences, defined as the training transitions associated with safety or task events. A fixed training log is replayed from the same initial Q-table under three conditions. FULL retains all updates, SKIP omits bad-experience updates, and NEUTRALIZE retains those updates without their event penalties. We illustrate the method on six classic Markov decision processes and evaluate it in closed-loop driving in CARLA. On the full driving task, FULL reached the goal from 33 of 40 test starts, whereas SKIP and NEUTRALIZE reached it from none, indicating that the bad experiences carry decision-relevant information. At the local level, for 47 decisions shortly before a failure of SKIP or NEUTRALIZE, removing or neutralizing a small group of bad experiences, with a median size of one, caused the trained agent to select the failing policy's action in 46 cases. For this agent, the results indicate that its decisions can be explained by its bad experiences.

##### **Data Attribution Should Steer Pretraining**
2611.19698v1 by Glenn Matlin, Chandreyi Chakraborty, Mohammed Rehan Parwani, Saehee Eom, Anthony Wen-Ming Zang, Akhil Theerthala, Arjun Chatterjee, Tejas Khandwekar, Brandon Michaels, Alvin Deng, Mark Riedl

Pretraining data curation decides what a model learns from, yet what makes a document worth learning from depends on what the model already knows. Influence-driven selectors already rank documents to improve one run; we argue that training-data attribution should steer pretraining by identifying testable data properties that become reusable curation criteria, evaluated on new data against cheaper selectors at matched total cost, discovery included. A document ranking is consumed by the run it was computed for, whereas an explained data property can be tested across corpora and checkpoints, especially where familiar quality signals diverge from measured usefulness. Because historical contribution, an understandable explanation, and future data value require distinct evidence, we specify a feedback loop that interprets attributed sources, tests candidate data changes, and states for five claim types what evidence each requires and what none establishes. We close with a falsifiable comparison against perplexity, classifier, and mixing baselines at equal total compute.

##### **Fairness Evaluation for Diffusion and Flow Models Should Account for the Generation Path: Implications for Clinical Imaging**
2611.28077v1 by Dharini Raghavan, Amritpal Singh, Anant Madabhushi

Medical image generation models are often evaluated using images produced by a single sampling procedure. However, diffusion and flow models can use different numbers of sampling steps, sampling schedules, or numerical solvers without changing their trained parameters. These changes may affect the generated images and the clinical measurements derived from them. We argue that fairness results obtained under one sampling procedure should not be assumed to hold under another, particularly when changes in sampling affect clinical task errors differently across patient groups. Building on previous work on medical image quality, accelerated sampling, generation dynamics, and generative model fairness, we examine why the sampling procedure should be considered part of the evaluated system. We define subgroup differences in clinical task error across sampling procedures and provide a mathematical example showing that two groups can have identical outputs under the exact dynamics and a reference sampler but different errors under an alternative sampler. We also extend this result to stochastic generation under shared noise. An exploratory experiment with an age-conditioned image generation model illustrates how changing the number and timing of sampling steps can alter image-derived measurements, although these differences do not establish clinical unfairness. We therefore argue that fairness evaluation should assess the sampling procedure used in practice and examine intermediate generation states when they help explain differences in clinically relevant errors across patient groups. Analysis code is available here https://github.com/rdharini2001/generation-path-fairness

##### **Lesion-Aware Explainable AI for Diabetic Retinopathy Grading from Fundus Images**
2611.19550v1 by Truong Thinh Nguyen, Hai Phong Nguyen, Truong Thanh Hung Nguyen, Giang Son Tran, Hung Cao

Post-hoc explanations of deep-learning models for diabetic retinopathy (DR) grading are difficult to trust because different attribution methods highlight different retinal regions for the same image, and a visually plausible heatmap does not show that the highlighted pixels drive the prediction. We propose the Consensus-Calibrated Explanation Map (CCEM), which fuses Grad-CAM++, Ada-SISE, and SmoothGrad Integrated Gradients (SG-IG) using image-specific weights derived from annotation-free reliability cues: deletion sensitivity, inter-method agreement, compactness, retinal containment, and peak structure. CCEM explains a frozen ordinal grader, an EfficientNet-B7 with CBAM and a scalar regression head, pre-trained on EyePACS 2015 in Stage 1 and fine-tuned on APTOS 2019 in Stage 2 with quadratic weighted kappa (QWK)-optimized thresholds; it reaches a QWK of 0.920 on the hidden Kaggle test set and 0.559 on the external MAPLES-DR cohort. Explanations are evaluated on MAPLES-DR, 198 MESSIDOR images with expert pixel-level lesion annotations that are never used for training, threshold fitting, or fusion. On the 193 images with annotated lesions, CCEM obtains the highest Soft Pointing Game score (69.61%; 95% CI 63.7-75.3), defined as the ratio of the strongest lesion-supported saliency to the global map maximum rather than a lesion detection rate, together with the lowest deletion AUC (0.570) and the best deletion-insertion balance (0.115); each gain is significant against the strongest single explainer on paired tests. Annotation-free, per-image reliability weighting therefore turns three disagreeing explainers into one map whose peak evidence is more lesion-supported and more faithful to the model than that of any single explainer.

##### **Objectify, Don't Delete: Complaint-Derived Evaluation of Ambient Clinical Notes**
2611.19525v1 by Nikita Gupta, Narayanan Unny Edakunni, Vikram Okaly, Suranjana Datta, Navya Penubadi, Karl Eric Swanson, Siddharth Sharma

Evaluating the quality of AI-generated clinical notes is hard: standard metrics like ROUGE measure lexical similarity to reference notes, while expert checklists encode what committees believe matters. Neither captures what clinicians actually complain about. Recent work has shown that evaluation criteria can be mined from user feedback and filtered to criteria that LLM judges can reliably enforce. We argue that this is where much of the value is lost. The criteria that fail enforceability are disproportionately the ones clinicians raise most often, because high-frequency complaints are stated as several questions glued together. No yes/no judgment can grade such composites as posed. We introduce an objectification step that rewrites such criteria into sets of scoped, determinate decisions. We demonstrate the approach through an evaluation pipeline that mines criteria from complaints, objectifies them, and deploys LLM judges against both planted defects and production notes. Across eleven defects planted into ACI-bench reference notes, objectified judges localize violations with median recall of 0.95, while reference metrics change by at most 0.037. Given the complaint as one broad question, the same judges still find every planted defect, but they also flag 70% of the unperturbed reference notes; one section-pair question per call raises Youden's J from 0.30 to 0.72 (by 0.19 to 0.26 with prompt effort matched). We release ACI-Defect, a perturbation benchmark; clinician-reviewed instance-level labels showing quality gaps in the ACI-bench gold standard (32 of 40 notes contain hallucinations; 40 of 40 omit discussed facts); and grounding-judge verdicts. On 1,446 production notes, mean instance-rate burden falls at every clinician-rating step (rho=-0.15), a small association. Defect counts have to be kept per span rather than collapsed into one pass/fail bit per note.

##### **LABEL: Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits in Drug Knowledge and Language Models**
2611.19166v1 by S M Rayeed, Charles V. Stewart, Yao Ma

Every medication comes with two types of medical facts: indications, which describe what conditions the drug treats (benefits), and contraindications, which describe who must avoid it to prevent serious harm (safety limits). Although contraindications are critical for patient safety and FDA regulations require listing them as rigorously as indications, we find that contraindications are far less documented than indications in general prose. In this paper, we measure how often these texts contain both facts across 574 drugs from PrimeKG by comparing a drug's indications against its own contraindications. Across Wikipedia articles and PubMed abstracts, we find that reference prose names a drug's benefits about twice as often as its safety limits (within-drug odds ratio 2.19). We call this documentation gap LABEL (Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits). Language models mirror this gap. As we scale Qwen2.5 from 0.5B to 14B, accuracy on indications rises by 18.3 points, whereas accuracy on contraindications remains flat near random guessing. However, when we provide models with a retrieved passage that states the answer, they use safety facts just as effectively as benefits. Furthermore, routing safety questions to official FDA labels boosts accuracy by 15.8 points whenever the label states the fact. Overall, our findings show that models fail on medication safety not because they cannot reason about risk, but because free-text corpora lack safety facts. Clinical AI systems should therefore route medication-safety questions to official regulatory labels and structured databases rather than relying on general public records. Code and data are available at https://github.com/SMRayeed/LABEL.

##### **From Sensors to Silicon: Causal Cross-Layer Self-Healing Edge AI for Secure and Energy-Adaptive IoT Systems**
2611.19165v1 by Mostafa Darvishi

The migration of artificial intelligence from centralized cloud infrastructure to resource-constrained Internet-of-Things (IoT) devices reduces communication latency and improves data locality, but it also tightly couples sensing quality, inference behavior, microarchitectural activity, hardware reliability, security, and energy management within the same physical node. Existing edge-intelligence solutions predominantly address these dimensions in isolation, making it difficult to determine whether an observed degradation originates from sensor malfunction, data-distribution shift, malicious execution, hardware faults, or resource and thermal stress. This paper presents a causal cross-layer self-healing Edge-AI framework that continuously observes synchronized telemetry spanning sensor health, inference uncertainty, processor hardware-performance counters, accelerator and memory activity, power and thermal state, and communication conditions. A temporally informed causal reasoning engine estimates the most probable origin of abnormal behavior and quantifies diagnostic uncertainty, including under concurrent perturbations.

##### **CONTEXT-PCa: Evaluating the role of clinical context in MRI-based AI for detecting clinically significant prostate cancer (study protocol)**
2611.19082v1 by Shiqi Huang, Weixi Yi, Shaheer Ullah Saeed, Yipei Wang, Wen Yan, Natasha Thorley, Shonit Punwani, Aqua Asif, Alexander Ng, Veeru Kasivisvanathan, Mark Emberton, Yipeng Hu

The objective of this study is to investigate the role of clinical variables - their breadth, source, and quality - in the development, utility, and generalisability of an AI system for bi-parametric MRI (bpMRI)-based diagnosis of clinically significant prostate cancer (csPCa). Clinical context is added to the published PI-CAI-2B image-only model, whose weights are fixed. The study cohort comprises 6,022 biparametric MRI cases from 4,540 patients at University College London Hospitals NHS Foundation Trust (2019-2024), of which 4,834 cases (3,632 patients) are held out for internal testing. Forty-three clinical variables are drawn from structured health records, clinical letters, MRI reports, and automated image measurements. The primary endpoint is the difference in the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) between the image-context and image-only models on the internal test set. Secondary analyses compare combining strategies, the contribution of individual variables, and performance across subgroups. All models are further tested on five external cohorts from primary diagnosis and population-based screening.

##### **MemoryLane: Supporting Reminiscence Conversations Between Older Adults with Mild Dementia and Lay Caregivers Using a Simulated AI Agent**
2611.19013v1 by Soumay Agarwal, Aaryan Gajula, Lingyao Li, Renkai Ma, Jennifer Martin, Krisstina Madan, Ellen Brown, Chen Chen

Reminiscence Therapy (RT) is a non-pharmacological intervention that invites patients to discuss and review past life experiences and memories to improve their emotional and mental well-being. In practice, many reminiscence conversations are led by lay caregivers, such as spouses and adult children. While traditional RT often relies on personal photographs, curating them can be tedious and difficult. In addition, caregivers who lack shared life experiences with the patient may struggle to facilitate engaging reminiscence conversations. This paper presents MemoryLane, an AI-integrated tool that helps lay caregivers facilitate reminiscence conversations without curated photographs. MemoryLane supports a triadic conversation among the patient, the caregiver, and a simulated AI agent that acts as the patient's peer. A shared interface shows the agent and AI-generated images of the memories being discussed, while a private smartphone interface lets the caregiver control the behavior of the simulated AI agent and moderate the AI-generated images. A preliminary focus group study with five dementia-care stakeholders suggested the value of MemoryLane's AI agent as a peer companion and of AI-generated images, as well as the feasibility of having caregivers moderate images and control the AI agent.

##### **Effects of Conventional, Adaptive, and AI-Powered Upscaling on the Legibility of License Plates**
2611.18949v1 by Hany Farid, Joshua D. Bucci, Emily A. Cooper

Small details in digital images -- a face, a street sign, a license plate -- are routinely upscaled in hopes of making them interpretable. Upscaling options now range from conventional interpolation, whose operations are spatially invariant and fully explainable, to AI-powered methods that synthesize detail potentially absent from the source. Here, we ask which of these techniques most reliably helps human readers. In two online experiments, we measured legibility of 255 US license plates ranging in resolution from 128 to 8 pixels tall, as well as their legibility after upscaling by eight different methods. Legibility of the un-upscaled plates collapsed from ceiling to floor across a narrow band of plate heights (32 to 10 pixels). Conventional upscaling techniques (bilinear, bicubic, and Lanczos interpolation) gave modest gains of about 5-9 percentage points within this range. Nearest-neighbor interpolation, however, was harmful, rendering unreadable many plates that were readable without any upscaling. Advanced methods diverged: SwinIR, an open-source AI enhancer, raised performance by up to 38 points relative to no upscaling, while an adaptive denoiser-based image prior and commercial AI upscaler tended to impair legibility. We found no strong evidence of consistent character hallucinations across methods. Decomposing the task, conventional interpolators mainly helped readers count characters, whereas SwinIR alone made the characters themselves more identifiable.

##### **Self-Improvement Memory Enables Continual Recursive Self-Improvement**
2611.18856v1 by Xin Yu, Fei Xia, Jiaqi Pan, Youbao Tang, Chengzhi Liu, Adams Yu, Sicong Jiang, Yanfei Chen, Lingzhou Xue, Ting Yu

Harness optimization is often studied with a fixed policy model and benchmark. When either changes, the optimizer must decide which parts of an evolved harness to reuse or revise, but the harness alone may not explain why earlier edits were made or how they performed. In this work, we study Continual Recursive Self-Improvement (CRSI), a setting for continuing existing harness-optimization methods across compatible benchmarks or policy models. We propose Self-Improvement Memory (SIM) for the harness optimizer, rather than the task-solving policy. SIM groups edits by recurring harness problem and records their rationale, predicted and observed outcomes, and evaluation context. New attempts extend this history as optimization continues. In our experiments across four domains, SIM achieves higher final scores than harness-only and root-cause-only continuation in cross-dataset and pairwise cross-model comparisons. Across ten independent source runs in coding, paired continuations with SIM improve final scores by an average of 2.36 points over harness-only continuation ($p=0.003$).
