##### **Can Mechanistic Interpretability Help Explain LLM Agent Failures? A Case Study on Web Element Hallucination**
2611.18514v1 by Mohamed Aghzal, Mor Geva, Gregory J. Stein, Ziyu Yao

Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) has made significant progress in explaining the internal computations of Large Language Models (LLMs); however, these advances have yet to demonstrate comparable success in explaining the decision-making processes of LLM-based agents. This paper examines the challenges MI tools face when applied to realistic agentic failures. We focus on one such failure: web agents hallucinating actions that are not available on a website. We apply a series of mechanistic analyzes to Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct as it interacts with live websites. We find that a linear probe trained on the last prompt token can predict hallucinations before generation begins (0.80 AUC) and steering along a probe direction corrects 85\% of hallucinations, but disrupts 7\% of valid actions. However, these results only provide a partial explanation of why the agent hallucinates. The probe direction is sensitive to prompt formatting, losing much of its steering effect after a minor change to how actions are represented. Additionally, circuit identification can be difficult as the computation is distributed across multiple components and positions, and the information the agent relies on varies across examples. We discuss how these observations challenge the assumptions underlying standard MI methods for circuit discovery and feature identification, and outline future directions for making these methods better suited to explain failures in LLM-based agents.

##### **From AI-Enabled Remote Patient Monitoring Toward Agentic Robotics: Embodiment, Governance, and Care Integration**
2611.18484v1 by Ivana Ragusa, Lorenzo Guiducci, Filippo Cavallo

AI-enabled remote patient monitoring is evolving beyond passive data collection toward agentic AI systems that interpret patient states, coordinate care, and initiate action across domestic and clinical settings. This structured scoping review finds that mature agentic care has not yet emerged: Most systems remain human led, while stronger decision-action coupling and embodied AI remain largely confined to prototypes and pilots. Healthcare robotics makes the transition from monitoring to action especially visible by translating AI-generated recommendations and decisions into speech, movement, prompting, physical assistance, escalation, and care coordination, making embodiment, clinical integration, human oversight, and governance fundamental design requirements. Further progress requires safe and interoperable care architectures in which intelligent components operate through clinically validated and accountable workflows. We propose agentic robotics as a future direction for supervised systems embedded in the care ecosystem, connecting AI agents and robots with clinicians, caregivers, and patients through auditable workflows that can be interrupted and remain subject to human oversight and explicit AI governance.

#### Abstracts
##### **ToolSmith: An Automatic Tool-Generation Framework for Multimodal Search Agents**
2611.31198v1 by Rui Sun, Junhua Huang, Luca Zancato, Md Mofijul Islam, Kai-Wei Chang

Recent multimodal search agents use reasoning, vision, and live web search across many turns to answer questions beyond parametric knowledge by orchestrating a small library of hand-crafted search and visual tools. However, the library is laborious to create and maintain, and frozen when new query types, evidence formats, and failure modes are encountered in the wild. Human practitioners, by contrast, cope with insufficient tools by fashioning new ones from familiar parts and reusing them across tasks. Inspired by this, we propose ToolSmith, an automatic tool-generation framework that grows and reuses modules on top of a lean base tool library. We evaluate ToolSmith on three in-domain multimodal search benchmarks and ten out-of-domain benchmarks under a VLM-as-a-Judge protocol. ToolSmith consistently improves over the base library across deployed agents of varying scales, with larger backbones reaping greater gains, demonstrating the scalability of the approach. Our code and data are available at https://threesr.github.io/toolsmith/.

摘要：

##### **SRCWDNet: Semantic-Relational Cross-Window Dual-Graph Network for Multi-Label Remote Sensing Scene Classification**
2611.31135v1 by Fareeha Amir

Multi-label remote sensing scene classification remains a challenging task due to complex spatial-spectral patterns, extreme class imbalance, and inter-label dependencies that are often insufficiently modeled. To address these challenges, we introduce a Semantic-Relational Cross-Window Dual-Graph Network (SRCWDNet), which integrates three key modules: (1) a Cross-Window Attention module that captures local and global visual context through learnable global tokens; (2) a Dual Label-Graph module that adaptively combines statistical co-occurrence graphs and learned semantic-embedding graphs to facilitate label-relational reasoning; and (3) a Neural Classifier Chain that sequentially predicts labels to exploit inter-label dependencies. Evaluated using five-fold cross-validation and trained with an Asymmetric-Graph-Consistency loss, SRCWDNet achieves accuracies of 98.93% and 96.53%, with corresponding F1-scores of 0.9365 and 0.8723 on the MLRSNet and China-MAS50k datasets, respectively, outperforming CNN, transformer, and lightweight baselines. These findings demonstrate that jointly modeling visual attention and adaptive label correlations can substantially improve multi-label remote sensing scene classification, particularly under long-tailed class distributions.

摘要：

##### **Self-Upgrading Embodied Agents**
2611.31117v1 by Jiajun Fan, Neil He, Qinglong Zheng, Changyi Yang, Tong Wei, Andy Mao, Zetian Li, Chaoran Cheng, Hui Ren, Ruihan Guo, Xianrui Zhong, Xizheng Yu, Jingyuan Li, Xi Zhu, Kaiqu Liang, Chang Xu, Zun Qian, Zidi Xiong, Yuezhu Zhao, Xu Huang, Ge Liu

An embodied agent built on a language model goes to work with the weights it was given and keeps them for life. To get better, it needs more training, or reflection, memory, search or prompts that cost every step and are lost when the task changes. Editing weights by reasoning, weight-space programming, could end this, but has stayed with models that only reason or relied on a far larger commercial model. We introduce the self-upgrading embodied agent, to our knowledge the first that learns from its own experience by upgrading its own weights on the job, between its tasks, with no training, no gradient, no reward model, no second model, no test-time scaling and no harness or prompt engineering. By day a 27B agent works in a household environment. At night it reads its record, finds many of its steps wasted on replies that reach the token limit before any action, traces the habit to one direction in its hidden states, and rewrites one tensor of its weights. An agent that rewrites itself must first never break itself, so it is its own examiner: it tests every change against its unchanged self on practice tasks and keeps only what holds. When an edit holds, the next day it is a better agent, at the same cost per step: over thousands of held-out episodes, 48 fresh seeds a task, its one kept edit makes it complete significantly more tasks, in fewer steps, with fewer wasted and fewer tokens. This self-improvement is multi-round, stable and continual: through the nights that follow, the upgrade holds, without collapse. Of four sizes of its family, only the 27B agent does this. From here on, an embodied agent need not keep training and inference apart: as it works, it gathers its own experience and upgrades itself, with no separate pre-training or post-training and no human intervention. We believe this opens lifelong continual learning for robots: stable, continual, recursive self-improvement.

摘要：

##### **RUIBench: What Makes a Good Robot-Use Interface?**
2611.31115v1 by Yanzhe Chen, Zhijun Cao, Zechen Bai, Wenzheng Zeng, Kevin Yuchen Ma, Yiqi Lin, Guoqiang Liang, Xiaokang Liu, Ke Sun, Kevin Qinghong Lin, Mike Zheng Shou

Foundation models can reason about complex physical tasks, yet translating this intelligence into reliable robot execution remains challenging. We argue that a critical, often overlooked factor is the robot-use interface (RUI), which determines how agents express and execute physical actions. To systematically study its impact, we introduce RUIBench, comprising 30 manipulation tasks across five suites spanning world knowledge, in-context learning, discovery, dynamic interaction, and contact-rich manipulation. We further develop RUIKit, a unified framework that crosses six action abstractions with two interaction forms, yielding 13 interfaces under shared perception and control. In simulation, changing only the RUI shifts a frontier agent's success rate from 45% to 81%, and that of a fine-tuned 4B model from 13% to 47%. Our analysis reveals that effective interfaces reduce unnecessary decisions while preserving essential feedback, with their benefits shaped by agent behavior and physical uncertainty. We further uncover a verification gap, where agents frequently declare success despite observable failures, revealing the need for task-level verification beyond individual actions. Together, these findings establish RUI design as a critical factor in embodied agent reliability.

摘要：

##### **AlphaDefect: Expert-Level AI for Slay the Spire 2**
2611.31076v1 by Qiyue Chen, Taian Yu, Junpei Komiyama, Haobo Huang

Slay the Spire 2 (STS2) is a single-player deck-building game that requires long-term planning under heavy randomness. We present AlphaDefect, an expert-level AI system for STS2: for each character, one network trained with proximal policy optimization (PPO) makes every decision of a run, in combat and outside it. At the highest difficulty, Ascension 10, on two versions of the game current in November 2026 and on test seeds not played in training, AlphaDefect wins 62-77% of runs across six characters with greedy play and 68-80% with test-time search, after 1.3 to 6.7 billion decisions of training per character; runs uploaded by human players of all levels are won 11-20% of the time at the same difficulty. The networks read the game as entity records that identify each card, relic and enemy through a learned embedding and attributes from the game data, together with consequences of public information computed by the engine. Ablations show how much the agent depends on this encoding: without these identities, the network did not win a single run, and without the computed consequences it won 8.3 points fewer. To our knowledge, AlphaDefect is the strongest publicly reported STS2 agent as of November 2026.

摘要：

##### **From Local Errors to System Failures: Modeling and Benchmarking Error Propagation in Multi-Agent LLM Systems**
2611.31063v1 by Wenlong Yu, Bingxu Lu, Qilong Wang, Qinghua Hu, Qianru Sun

In multi-agent systems (MAS) built on large language models (LLMs), errors can propagate through intermediate messages and compromise final answers. However, outcome-based evaluation obscures how agents amplify, attenuate, or correct these errors. We propose Response-Aware Error Propagation among Agents (REPA), a structural model that connects measured local responses to system-level error propagation. REPA separately estimates input assembly and agent processing across multiple error channels, while theoretical analysis relates local modeling errors to endpoint prediction error. Experiments span three Qwen base models, six MAS workflows, and ten knowledge and reasoning settings in ErrPropBench-10. REPA achieves a mean condition-level Spearman correlation of 0.84 across base models, compared with 0.47 for direct regression. Controlled experiments also demonstrate its utility for targeted recovery. REPA-guided retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves accuracy over no retrieval by an average of 19 percentage points with 16% additional tokens. By linking local agent behavior to system susceptibility, REPA provides an interpretable foundation for studying error propagation and developing targeted optimization in MAS.

摘要：

##### **DKnown-Jev-2: Article-Level Answer-Evidence Classification and Efficient Inference with a Pre-trained Language Model**
2611.31056v1 by Qi Li, Jianjun Xu, Jiu Li, Pingtao Wei

Evidence selection in normative knowledge question answering requires determining whether a candidate article contains content that directly supports an answer under the applicable conditions of the question. We present DKnown-Jev-2, an article-level answer evidence classifier designed for evidence integration across multiple paragraphs. The model jointly encodes question conditions, article metadata, and paragraph content, and uses a pointer-style classification head to predict Answer Evidence (AE) and Non-answer Evidence (NE) in a single forward pass. Ona manually annotated test set of 972 evaluation instances, DKnown-Jev-2 achieves 91.77% accuracy and 91.28% macro-F1, outperforming Jev by 2.16 and 2.37 percentage points, respectively, while reducing both evidence misses and non-evidence false positives. The test set contains 317 multi-paragraph instances, with a maximum body length of 9,322 characters. Shared article encoding and non-autoregressive classification support low-latency inference. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method for article-level evidence selection in normative knowledge question answering.

摘要：

##### **LimeTree: Linear-Memory Graph Neural Networks over Tree-Graphs for Resource-Efficient Language Modeling and Context Extrapolation**
2611.30934v1 by Elisabeth Fink

The search for computationally efficient alternatives to the Transformer has yielded numerous architectures in recent years; however, most exhibit severe weaknesses in associative recall-such as key-value (KV) retrieval-and length extrapolation, where even standard Transformers struggle. This paper introduces LimeTree, a linear-complexity architecture in both memory and compute for sequence modeling. By integrating synthetic KV retrieval tasks directly into standard language modeling training, LimeTree achieves strong perplexity alongside reliable recall. Two variants were evaluated on WikiText-103 and PG-19 under strict compute constraints. On WikiText-103, LimeTree reaches a token-level perplexity of 19 in just 6 hours on four Quadro RTX 8000 GPUs, while maintaining 100\% KV retrieval accuracy even when extrapolating from a training context length of 512 to an evaluation length of 16,384. On PG-19, the model achieves a token-level perplexity of 13 in 35 hours on a single RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU with minimal hyperparameter tuning. These results position LimeTree as a competitive linear-complexity alternative to Transformers, combining superior perplexity and training throughput with robust in-context retrieval and length extrapolation.

摘要：

##### **Unpacking the Evaluators: How Configuration Shapes the Evaluation of Alignment in Explainable AI**
2611.30816v1 by Gizem Karagoz, Tanir Ozcelebi, Nirvana Meratnia

Explainable AI (XAI) evaluation fundamentally requires human-centered evaluation, assessing whether explanations align with human reasoning and expert knowledge. However, such evaluations often require expert-driven manual validation and are difficult to scale, leading to the widespread use of alignment-based metrics, where attribution maps are compared against predefined ground-truth (GT) representations. Despite their popularity, alignment evaluations are implicitly bias towards multiple design choices made during the evaluation process, for example GT representations, metric families, metric formulations (soft vs. hard), and thresholding strategies. In this paper, we formalize alignment evaluation as a structured evaluation configuration space through which the influence of these design choices can be systematically analyzed. We further introduce a structured alignment evaluation framework that models interactions between XAI methods, GT representations, metric families, metric formulations, and thresholding strategies across 2D and 3D synthetic and real-world datasets. In addition, we propose a novel taxonomy of XAI output behavior based on local entropy and boundary mass ratio, enabling structured characterization of attribution patterns for alignment evaluation. Our experiments reveal systematic flows (triples of XAI-GT-metric configurations), showing that alignment outcomes provide distinct behavior patterns under certain configurations, such as soft/hard metric implementations. We further observe varying levels of threshold sensitivity across metric families and attribution structures. These findings demonstrate that alignment evaluation cannot be treated as a fixed or invariant measure of explanation quality, and provide practical guidance for designing more reliable and behavior-aware XAI evaluation protocols.

摘要：

##### **PVFormer: Proper Velocity Transformer for Stable and Scalable Hyperbolic Representation Learning**
2611.30785v1 by Xianglong Shi, Nicu Sebe, Bernhard Schölkopf, Ziheng Chen

Hyperbolic representations have shown remarkable success on hierarchical and tree-like data, and Transformers have become a central architecture for representation learning. However, a principled construction of hyperbolic Transformers remains challenging because the self-attention layer decomposes into three coupled primitives, transformation, similarity, and aggregation, that should all respect the underlying geometry. Existing hyperbolic Transformer designs are typically built in the Poincaré or Lorentz models, where bounded domains, time-like constraints, tangent-space detours, or projection steps make it difficult to keep the full block intrinsic and scalable. To address this gap, we propose PVFormer, to the best of our knowledge, the first Transformer architecture that operates natively in proper velocity (PV) space. The proper velocity model provides an unconstrained representation of hyperbolic geometry, and PVFormer exploits this structure to redesign the Transformer block in PV space. On the transformation side, we use PV homomorphism transformations to update query and value representations. On the similarity side, we generalize Euclidean inner-product attention with a PV Busemann score inspired by Busemann-based hyperbolic learning and a PV point-to-hyperplane key transformation. On the aggregation side, we replace Euclidean averaging with PV midpoint aggregation and further derive a scalable PV linear attention variant for large graphs. Extensive experiments support the effectiveness of PVFormer across graph, text, and vision benchmarks.

摘要：

##### **CocoonBench: Can Multilingual Search Agents Escape Language Information Cocoons?**
2611.30727v1 by Hongchuan Zeng, Bowen Liu, Changjiang Gao, Senyu Han, Zhipei Xu, Peisong Wang, Xinbing Liang, Yuhan Li, Nuo Chen, Blaze Chen

Multilingual search agents can access the global Web, yet may remain trapped in a language environment with insufficient evidence; we refer to this failure as a language information cocoon. However, existing search benchmarks largely operate within a single language, especially English, overlooking entity connections across multiple languages. We introduce CocoonBench, a benchmark that evaluates whether multilingual search agents can escape such language information cocoons. CocoonBench mines answer-centered relational paths from a frozen Wikidata-derived graph and selects clue sets that are individually necessary and jointly identify a unique answer. Using language-specific entity names, it records each task's minimum language cover, removes direct identifiers, and renders each task as matched prompts in three languages together with its construction certificate and provenance. Across 10 state-of-the-art models, mean accuracy on CocoonBench ranges from 33.8% (DeepSeek-V4-Flash) to 69.7% (Claude-Opus-4.8), leaving even the strongest system below 70%. The same construction certificates support path-aligned trajectory analysis, enabling diagnosis of model failures in language routing, evidence-path recovery, and answer use. The result shows that escaping a language cocoon requires more than switching languages: successful cross-lingual search depends on both covering the correct languages and connecting the evidence found across them.

摘要：

##### **HOSE: Hierarchical Orthogonal Subspace Erasure for Concept Unlearning in Diffusion Models**
2611.30592v1 by Xiaoqi Wei, Li Li, Kejiang Chen, Hanzhou Wu, Xinpeng Zhang

Text-to-image diffusion models can generate harmful or sensitive content, such as pornographic or violent imagery, raising serious security concerns. Model editing has recently emerged as an efficient approach to mitigate this issue. It modifies pretrained models with minimal parameter updates to alter specific concepts while preserving overall model capabilities. However, due to the entangled representations of different concepts in diffusion models, direct modifications inevitably disrupt non-target concepts. This method projects parameter updates into the subspace orthogonal to general knowledge, thereby eliminating interference with non-target concepts. Considering the hierarchical heterogeneity of the U-Net architecture, we further propose a hierarchical adaptive mechanism, constructing differentiated subspace projections for different layers to precisely adapt to the model's hierarchical structure, thereby resolving the inherent trade-off between erasure and image quality in unified constraints. Experimental results demonstrate the proposed method reduces harmful content generation by 80% compared with the unedited model. While it inevitably incurs some image quality degradation, it still outperforms other editing-based baselines in terms of quality preservation, and achieves a significantly better trade-off between harmful content suppression and quality loss than these baselines. Notably, our approach demonstrates higher adversarial robustness than existing SOTA method, indicating that the target concepts have been more thoroughly erased. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/sevenqiiu/HOSE.

We trace the difference to the unit of sender identity: Elliptic2 senders are entity clusters that recur across many labeled subgraphs, whereas Elliptic++ senders are individual addresses, and fewer than half of test transactions have a sending address seen in training. 2611.29619v1 by Panagiotis Kasnesis, Lazaros Toumanidis, Amalia Contiero Syropoulou, Christos Chatzigeorgiou

Most LLM agent frameworks build a crew that runs a task and exits. We present Wactorz, an open-source runtime in which LLM-generated agents are long-lived, supervised actors: a planner turns a chat request into Python, compiles it into a new actor at runtime, and a supervisor restarts that actor alone, with its state intact, when it crashes. The recorded demo builds a camera-to-lamp automation from one chat request, adds a second agent that is auto-wired to the first by topic and carries a deliberate division-by-zero, watches that agent repair its own code without losing its count, then deploys a fresh Raspberry Pi node and live-migrates the agent onto it, in under four minutes of wall-clock time and \$0.13 of model usage. 2611.29475v1 by Ruiji Yu, Wenye Ma, Gus Xia

Musical style, a global attribute that functions throughout a whole piece, is a lever widely used by users in modern large music generation models. Yet how style is represented inside these models, and which components drive it, remains largely unexplored. In language models, circuit analysis traces a representation to a sparse sub-network of a few key components, namely attention heads and MLP neurons, and has been used successfully to explain and control global attributes such as emotion. In this work, we use circuit analysis to study the mechanisms behind musical style. Based on YuE2, we build a dataset that pairs the same lyrics with five styles and design a three-step framework. First, stage localization shows that the style forms mainly in the score-planning stage. Second, static observation across the positions of this stage further reveals that style is not a fixed vector but a dynamic representation across tokens. Third, we therefore merge the activation differences across positions into one direction per style, and trace the neurons and attention heads that drive this direction into a sparse circuit. Without any style text, steering this circuit successfully transfers one piece into the target style, matching and even exceeding the style prompt. ##### **Gaussian Belief Propagation and Periodic Jacobi Matrices on Trees: Spectral Curve, Antibound Poles, and Fixed Points**
2611.29381v1 by Yusuke Watanabe

Diagonal Green functions of a periodic Jacobi operator on a universal covering tree satisfy the scalar Gaussian belief propagation covariance equations on the finite connected loopless base multigraph. This proves the conjecture of Christiansen, Simon and Zinchenko. For positive weights the pole result includes self-loops. On loopless graphs the lower pole is the walk-summability threshold. Each energy strictly between it and the spectral bottom has at least two fixed points with positive definite local beliefs. On a connected simple graph, some coefficients yield a compact component of positive solutions exactly when the graph contains two vertex-disjoint connected subgraphs of cycle rank at least two. The covariance curve is generically irreducible, with one sheet per edge subset with no vertex of degree one. Then all eigenvalues of the signed Jacobi matrices are antibound poles. Signed principal submatrices classify finite points with nonzero residue of $(\sum_i V_i) dz$, where $V_i$ are the vertex variances. We give a genus formula. An intervention that improves one agent can break another, and adding a stronger harness on top need not repair the damage. We ask instead when each intervention helps and which failure it repairs. A matched factorial study over nine benchmarks, four models, four skill conditions, four harness levels, and three budgets, with nearly 0.9 million trajectories, changes one axis at a time. No configuration is best everywhere, interventions interact rather than add, and a harness can absorb a skill's gain, rescue a failure the skill causes, or leave the gain intact. Yet feedback is not recovery: a matched recovery repairs mainly failures that leave a visible error signal. 2611.29127v1 by Mark Heimann, Bruno Jacob

Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) weights every supervised token equally, so on knowledge-dense corpora the loss budget follows token count rather than importance: on a legal QA benchmark, citation strings absorb 81% of the supervised loss weight budget while the answers being taught absorb 5%. Trustworthy benchmarks must separate patients, source-linked evidence, and time: historical tests should use only knowledge available at the intended decision time.

We introduce LintBench, a human-validated benchmark of 113 real-world Android Lint checks, each paired with a natural language description and its original test suite as a held-out oracle. We evaluate four LLMs, including proprietary and open-weight models. We compare prompting strategies, then evaluate execution-guided repair with and without retrieved API knowledge. Prompting alone reaches 30%-65% (pass@5) across models. Execution-guided repair and retrieval reach 57%-85% test-passing rates within five calls. Our results show that execution feedback raises the pass rate, while retrieved framework knowledge raises the compile rate, which reaches up to 99%. The framework knowledge helps the low-performing models the most, providing the knowledge they need to fix compilation errors. Of the generated checks that compile but fail their tests, 87% have false negatives, missing an issue the generated check should report. Based on our findings, getting the issue-detection logic right remains an open challenge. We release LintBench and the evaluation harness to support future work on generating Android Lint checks.

##### **Search From the Start: Search-Native Pretraining for Language Models**
2611.26809v1 by Kaustubh Ponkshe, Gyung Hyun Je, Stefan Taga, Colin Raffel, Martin Jaggi

Modern language models (LMs) are increasingly deployed with access to search tools at inference. Yet pretraining remains predominantly focused on predicting static text, with search capabilities typically introduced during post-training. We propose Search-Native Pretraining as a practical route to teach search capability during pretraining from ordinary text corpora. Specifically, we augment pretraining corpus at scale with web-search queries and retrieve web response using a local index. Training on this augmented corpus teaches models to generate search calls and incorporate retrieved evidence during language modeling. Across Wikipedia and DCLM-Edu corpora and 360M to 3.3B parameter scales, search-pretrained models ("QueryLM") show improved performance on knowledge-intensive tasks and higher steerability toward the retrieved evidence. Crucially, their advantage persists after supervised fine-tuning of both search-pretrained and conventionally pretrained models, indicating that the tested post-training procedures do not fully recover the capabilities developed during pretraining. Throughout our experiments, we find that a 1.4B QueryLM consistently beats or matches the performance of a FLOP-matched 3.3B baseline model across all knowledge intensive tasks. These results establish search-pretraining as a practical way to develop foundational search capabilities during pretraining. We release the training artifacts, including model checkpoints and data.

##### **What Helps Language-Model Agents Review Violent Video? A Controlled Comparison with a Human Evidence Reference**
2611.26776v1 by Mohammed El Amine Mokhtari, Matei Mancas, Virginie Vandenbulcke

Systems that flag violent video for a human operator can now be built from language models without training, by combining perception models and letting a language model decide. We ask how the observations of the perception models should reach the decision, and which evidence matters. On the XD-Violence test set (799 clips) we fix one set of perception agents (video description, object detection, sound tagging, speech recognition and speech tagging) and one reasoning model, and compare three interfaces: messages between agents, a typed evidence graph explored with tools, and flat text read in one pass; and a single audio-visual model given the raw clip. Two annotators provide a human reference that records the decision, the incident segments and whether they relied on sight, sound or speech. Flat text matches the single model (F1 0.967 against 0.968), while the graph agent and message passing are less accurate; a variant of the graph agent developed on a separate set did not close the gap, and both graph summaries and multi-turn tool use lowered accuracy. These results hold with reworded prompts and with a larger reasoning model, except that the larger model weakens message passing enough for the graph agent to beat it. Sound events improve F1 by +0.012 [+0.004, +0.021], in line with the annotators, who used sound or speech on most violent clips. Citations of stored observations are faithful, changing about half of the decisions when removed, but no more so with the graph than with flat text, and the agent pipeline costs about 3 times the GPU time of the single model. We also report two artefacts of vision-language describers that mislead pipelines built on them.

##### **Know Your Context Bank: Repairing Inconsistency and Measuring Generalization in Context-Aware Text-to-SQL**
2611.26775v1 by Hailong Li, Mohammadreza Pourreza, Tal Neiman, Reza Sherkat, Per Jacobsson, Yannis Papakonstantinou

Text-to-SQL systems increasingly rely on context banks: curated collections of example queries, metric definitions, and domain knowledge that tell a model how an organization writes its queries. In the enterprise, a bank grows over years as many analysts add entries, checked one at a time for correctness (does the SQL answer its own question?) and rarely for consistency with one another. Yet correctness is often loosely defined: a question seldom fixes how an entity is counted or which table defines it, so two entries can each pass review yet resolve the same decision in opposite ways. We call this condition context bank rot. A second question follows: does a system that uses a bank generalize from it, or only reproduce it? Existing benchmarks cannot tell, as they do not control how far a test question sits from the context. We answer both questions by comparing queries decision by decision, with a fast scorer distilled from human-calibrated LLM judges. Turned inward, the comparison diagnoses and repairs a bank. Fine-tuning on a cleaned BIRD-train bank beats a same-size random subset at every epoch (+2.6 points on average), and 92.4% of our removals had their SQL corrected by an independent expert audit, against 45.6% of audited entries. In-context examples that follow one convention beat those following the opposite one by 13.3 points, while a mix is barely better than none. Turned outward, the comparison generates test questions at a controlled distance from a bank, a pipeline we call CAT (Context-Aware Testing). On CAT-BIRD, for retrieval and fine-tuning alike, the benefit of a bank falls step by step with distance and is largely gone once about three quarters of a question's logic is new, and on CAT-Spider the benefit of retrieval likewise shrinks with distance.
